Each segment cuts its own trough, a spacing across, into a plateau at the mean of what the troughs take (GROOVE_DEPTH_MEAN, the mean of the faults' depths), so that the lane keeps its level, two segments in a relay both show, and no seam marks where one gives way to the next. The grooves die out over GROOVE_MARGIN_SPACINGS in from the bounding faults and any fault across an end, so that they end in their own tips rather than on a line. A later pulse lays its troughs over the earlier set's where its own segments stand. Stated choices.
- **F133 Coronae over an ice mantle's upwellings.** Miranda had no preset, and the user asked whether its coronae, named as Venus's are, should come of Ganymede's lanes. They come of upwellings. A tidal heating episode switches convection on in an ice mantle on rock under its lid, and the upwellings of the new convection lift and stretch the lid (Hammond and Barr 2014). The episode is the tidal flux above the floor that renews an icy surface, as the lanes' is (F44); a lid over steady or waning convection, as Pluto's and Ceres's over their radiogenic heat, stays stagnant, so no corona marks it. The layer convects under the lid at the episode's thinnest, from the lid's base to the rock; a body with an ocean has none, its shell being the lid. Its cells are as wide in each limb as the layer is deep (CORONA_CELL_ASPECT), so that upwellings stand twice the layer's depth apart at its mid-depth, the spacing widening to the lid's base as the radius does, and as many as hexagonal cells of that spacing fit on the sphere there: `volcanism.corona_count`, five on Miranda, whose ice 145 km deep under a 14 km lid gives cells about 370 km apart (Hammond and Barr's models give the few plumes its coronae need, low orders standing where the core is under half the radius). The lid takes an upwelling's stretching in faults of its own where it bends over a shorter length than the upwelling is wide, its flexural parameter against the distance from the upwelling to its cell's edge, the share falling as one over one plus their ratio to the fourth power (CORONA_FLEXURE_POWER); times the episode's drive it is the coronae's `volcanism.corona_strength`, 0.99 on Miranda, and no corona is drawn under CORONA_STRENGTH_FLOOR. The coronae take their time from the episode as the lanes do (`volcanism.corona_age`, `volcanism.corona_duration`), each its own within it, and the lid of that time, from the thermal relations at that time rather than the history's samples, spaces its faults (GROOVE_NECKING_WAVELENGTH, GROOVE_RIFT_LID_SHARE, F61) and sets its strength. The upwellings are placed each the farthest from those placed of CORONA_PLACEMENT_TRIES tries. Each cell is bounded by the bisectors with its neighbours, a polygon, its corners rounded (CORONA_CORNER_SHARPNESS) and its outline wandering (CORONA_OUTLINE_WANDER over CORONA_OUTLINE_WANDER_SCALE), as Miranda's coronae are "polygonal to ovoidal". The corona is the part of the cell where the flow under the lid stretches it in area, out to where it turns to shortening it: for a flow that rises at the centre and sinks at the edge with a speed that rises and falls as half a sine across the cell, CORONA_OUTLINE_SHARE, 0.646 of the way. Its outer belt, CORONA_BELT_SHARE of its radius (Elsinore's 80 km), is a ring of the grooves' fault segments (F132) concentric with its outline, closing round it without a seam; its core is crossed by two sets at CORONA_CROSS_ANGLE, their segments less often (CORONA_CORE_LEVEL), turning across it by up to CORONA_CORE_TURN over CORONA_CORE_TURN_SCALE of its radius, where the lid stretched every way over the upwelling broke in more than one direction, the two changing over CORONA_CORE_BLEND. The lid is thinnest over the upwelling, the heat the flow brings up CORONA_FLUX_CONTRAST above the mean at the centre and as much below at the edge, so that the faults' spacing, as the lid's thickness, widens outward, as Inverness's does. The upwelling stretches the lid for as long as the episode lasts, far longer than its faults take to rotate, so its blocks tilt until their faults lock: from CORONA_FAULT_DIP_DEG, the dip Arden's faults broke at (Pappalardo et al. 1997), to CORONA_LOCKUP_DIP_DEG, where friction of about 0.6 locks a normal fault, new faults then taking the strain at the same tilt. A block tilted 20 degrees stands its width times sin 20 degrees from crest to trough, the middling fault's trough below its plateau: 1.7 km on Miranda's 4.9 km blocks against Arden's 2 km, where the lanes' share of their spacing, calibrated on Ganymede's grooves, gives a few hundred metres. Each trough is a tilt block's, its scarp facing outward and its back slope long (GROOVE_FINE_SKEW), as Arden's scarps face outward. Its bounding faults face outward too, their footwall, the corona's side, up and the ground beyond down, so that the belt stands above the old ground; beyond the outline the belt's faults run on as open fractures. The corona renews the ground inside its outline as a lane does (F60, F61), by its cover, its strength, on the loop's grid at its time and on the finish's, with its own unit (UNIT_CORONA), its ice clean and its troughs darker; it stretches and breaks the old ground across it, and keeps of the old relief only what is longer than CORONA_SMOOTH_SHARE of its radius. Stated choices, but for the outline's share, which is the flow's, and the dips, which are measured.
- **F133 An ice mantle frozen through makes no ice magma.** The magma rate of an ice mantle came of its heat flux whatever the lid, so Miranda, whose conductive profile would reach its melting point some 2,000 km down, eight times its radius, made ice magma and 187 domes. Ice melts only where the profile reaches its melting point inside the body: the excess the profile would reach at the centre over the melting point, (T_m - T_s)(R / L - 1), is taken against one rheological temperature scale of ice, R T_m² / E, as the rocky mantle's melt is against its own (F123), in the present rate and through the history. Miranda makes none today and its domes go; Ceres's and Pluto's profiles reach the melting point at depth, and theirs stand.
- **F133 No heat pipe in ice.** The tidal boost to the magma share, which reaches 0.9 of the heat at 1 W/m² (F17), is a silicate mantle's heat pipe, Io's, its melt lighter than its lid. It gave Miranda's episode, 62 mW/m² of tides, 5.6 percent of its heat as ice magma, which flooded 40 percent of the surface 1.9 km deep. An ice mantle's melt is denser than the ice over it, and its tides take no boost; Miranda's episode makes five small floods. The ocean worlds' magma rates fall with it (Europa's from 0.51 to 0.0012 km³/yr), which nothing they make reads: their shells do not flood.
- **F134 Relaxation keeps its bands; a large crater is rough down to the grid.** The user saw large craters on the icy moons with smoothed floors and concentric ridges spreading out like ripples, jagged at a larger scale than the ground about them. Three causes. The loop relaxed the surface band by band at every step, splitting the whole surface afresh into the pyramid's bands and scaling each by its survival; the bands overlap in wavelength, so a wavelength shared between a band that survives and one that relaxes lost its share of the second at every step: after many steps relief well below the shortest relaxing wavelength was gone too, and the repeated filter rang outward from every edge in concentric ripples, on the coarse grid's cells. The bands are now kept from step to step (`relaxation::Bands`): each step splits only what changed since the last, adds it to the bands the relief already holds, and scales those, so that every part of the relief decays in the bands it had when it came, by the product of their survivals. A crater whose long bands go and short bands hold, stepped sixty times, keeps its short bands to the metre and lays no ripple beyond two and a half radii (split afresh, it lost 569 m of a 1 km crater's kept relief and rang 40 m beyond). A crater's texture, its ejecta's hummocks and its floor's roughness, was value noise on lattices scaled to its radius in two octaves: on a crater of 250 km, hummocks 15 to 50 km across and floor lumps of 13 km with nothing finer; and the lattices' cubic fade let the curvature jump at every node, which on the hummocks' polar lattice drew concentric rings and spokes in the shaded relief. The lattices now fade by the quintic, whose curvature runs on across a node, and the texture goes on below them in octaves from CRATER_FINE_START cycles per radius down to two cells of the grid, each weaker by the ground's Hurst exponent, so that a large crater is rough at every scale the ground about it is. Stated choices.
- **F135 The bombardment of any star.** The user saw Enceladus and Miranda carry Mimas's crater population, Herschel-like basins and all, where their maps show fewer and smaller craters, and asked for a bombardment that would serve a planet close to a red dwarf as well as the Solar System, fitted to the Solar System's records. Every body was struck by the Moon's impactors, the Neukum production function and chronology from the time its crust solidified, at 0.1 times the Moon's flux times its planet's focusing beyond 4 AU: Enceladus took 2.2 times the Moon's craters of every size from 80 Myr after it formed, Miranda a crater wider than its radius 0.43 Gyr ago. A body's place against its star's snow line, x, its distance over the distance where a blackbody is 170 K (2.69 AU for the Sun, found from the insolation, so for any star), now sets which population strikes it, how hard and how fast (`cratering.snow_line_position`). Two populations. The inner system's rocky leftovers keep the Neukum production function and chronology, at the Moon's rate per unit area inside x = 0.40 and twice it from 0.57 through the belt (Mars, Ivanov 2001), fading out from 1.2 to 1.6, beyond which comets strike the giant planets' moons (Zahnle et al. 2003); the Mars production function takes over from 0.447 (1.2 AU). The outer system's planetesimals, scattered from beyond the giant planets, strike from the snow line out (their share ramping in from x = 1 to 1.5): their numbers fall with size as a cumulative power law of slope 2.1 below 100 km and 4.5 above (Nesvorný et al. 2023; Wong et al. 2023), and each makes a crater by the scaling for icy satellites, D_i = 0.143 D^1.09 g^0.28 (ρ_i/ρ_t)^−0.43 v^−0.56 D_sc^0.19 in km, m/s² and km/s (Zahnle et al. 2003, as Wong et al. 2023 use it): a comet of 1 km makes a crater of 23 km on Enceladus, 18 km on Ganymede and 6 km on Pluto. Unfocused, 4.7e−7 comets of 1 km or more strike a km² each Gyr inside x = 3.56 (Saturn), more as x² beyond, nearer the scattered disc, times the focusing of the planet a moon circles, or of the body itself: Nesvorný et al. 2023's rates on the moons of Jupiter, Saturn and Uranus, each over its planet's focusing, come to 5.3, 4.0 and 16.5 e−16 per km² per year, and the rule gives Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto, Enceladus and Miranda their rates within a factor of 1.3. The comets' flux declines from formation as a stretched exponential, exp(−(t / 1.6 Myr)^0.317) of it still to come (Wong et al. 2023's fit for Saturn), in all 2475 Gyr of today's flux: the 26e−3 and 18e−3 comets of 1 km per km² Wong et al. give Mimas and Enceladus over their whole history, over today's rates, are about 550 times 4.5 Gyr, as the Neukum chronology's early bombardment is about 500 times the Moon's. They strike at the share of the orbital speed about the star that falls from 0.5 at the Earth's place to 0.4 by the snow line, a moon adding three times the square of its own orbital speed (Zahnle et al. 2003): Io 31, Europa 24, Ganymede 20, Callisto 15, Enceladus 22 and Miranda 12 km/s, within 7 percent of Nesvorný et al.'s. Around another star the leftovers clear on its own orbital times: the early bombardment's timescales go as the orbital period at the snow line, (L/L☉)^0.75 (M/M☉)^−0.5, and its amount per unit area as the disc's solids, which go as the star's mass, over the area within the snow line, M/L (stated choices); the steady flux after it depends on belts and giant planets the engine does not model, and is `cratering.late_flux_multiplier` times the Solar System's at the same place, the early bombardment kept as it is. A planet two thirds of the Earth's insolation from an ultracool dwarf, as TRAPPIST-1e, meets its rocky leftovers at 26 km/s (Kral et al. 2018: 15 to 110), and its early bombardment, decaying over 1.7 Myr, is over long before its crust solidifies, so that it keeps a record of the late flux alone. A body holds a crater as wide as its radius if icy (Odysseus 0.84 R on Tethys, Turgis 0.79 R on Iapetus, Herschel 0.70 R on Mimas) and as wide as itself if rocky (Vesta kept Rheasilvia, 1.93 R, and older craters beside it, Schenk et al. 2012; since F168 as wide as a hemisphere, as Mars kept Borealis); a larger impact shatters and resurfaces it, and its record starts at the median time of the last such impact, when ln 2 are still to come, or when its crust solidifies if later (`cratering.record_start`). The loop draws craters to the hold, a rocky body's beyond 1.5 radii, the stamp's limit, as mega-impacts (F168). Mimas's record then starts 4.09 Ga, near its plains' 4.16 to 4.21 Ga (Wong et al. 2023), and holds 1.31e−3 craters of 10 km per km² and 2.68e−4 of 20 km against the 1.38e−3 and 2.85e−4 counted (Kirchoff and Schenk's counts as Wong et al. give them); Enceladus's starts 4.25 Ga and Miranda's 4.20 Ga; Ceres's 3.97 Ga, after which it holds 13 craters over 100 km against the 16 counted (Marchi et al. 2016); the Moon, Mercury, Venus, the Earth, Io and the large icy moons keep their record from when their crust solidified; Mars's starts 3.95 Ga, its rocky flux, twice the Moon's with the Mars production function continued to basins, giving it two impacts wider than itself after its crust solidified. The comets' slope runs on below 1 km: Singer et al. 2019 found few craters under 13 km on Pluto and Charon, a deficit of comets under 1 to 2 km, but the moons of Saturn keep the steep slope down to craters of 2 km (Kirchoff and Schenk's counts), and with the deficit Mimas held a third of its craters of 10 km and Enceladus a tenth of its craters of 2 km (stated choice; Wong et al. 2023 date the moons either way). Jupiter's moons record fewer small craters than Saturn's, Zahnle et al. 2003's case A against their case B; one population everywhere gives them Saturn's. The gardening diffusivity follows the body's present production of craters of 1 km against the Moon's, the small impacts that do the gardening coming as often: about the Moon's on Mars, Ceres and Pluto, 1.9 times it on Callisto, 4 on Ganymede, 17 on Enceladus, whose craters of 1 km need comets of 33 m.
- **F136 The comets' sizes from their collisional history, craters under strength, and a shattered moon's debris.** F135 gave the comets one power law, cumulative slope 2.1 up to 100 km, and made their craters by Zahnle et al. 2003's scaling for icy satellites, 23 to 28 times the comet on Enceladus and Mimas; normalized on Mimas's counts at 10 and 20 km, it gave Mimas 33 craters of 50 km per 10⁶ km² against the 23 counted (Kirchoff and Schenk's counts as Bottke et al. 2024 replot them), and Mimas-like moons twice their basins. The comets now follow the size distribution of the destabilized Kuiper belt after its collisional history (Bottke et al. 2024, Fig. 1, read off the curve: the distribution their craters on Iapetus and Phoebe require, to 32 km, joined to their model beyond, to 300 km): a cumulative slope of about 2 at tens of metres, 1.2 from 0.1 to 0.6 km, as steep as 2.5 in the bump of collision fragments from 2 to 6 km, 1.7 to 2 from 10 to 60 km and 5 from 130 to 300 km; 2.7 below the table (the smallest, weakest bodies, Bottke et al. 2024) and 2.5 above (Bottke et al. 2023). Each makes its crater by pi-scaling with the target's strength, D_tc = k d [g d / (2 v²) + (Y / (rho v²))^((2 + mu) / 2)]^(-mu / (2 + mu)) (Holsapple and Housen 2007, as Bottke et al. 2024 write it), k 1.1 and mu 0.55 for cold ice (Holsapple 2022), Y its 35 MPa or 7.6 MPa for rock (Holsapple 1993's soft rock, stated choice), the transient cavity collapsing by McKinnon and Schenk's law: under its strength a 1 km comet makes a crater of 18 km on Mimas, 17 on Enceladus, 12 on Miranda and 19 on Ganymede. With F135's fluxes, histories and hold rule unchanged and nothing fitted, Mimas, Tethys and Dione keep their counts from 20 km up within 1.4 times, Tethys's to 400 km; their craters under 20 km fall short, to 0.36 of Mimas's at 3.5 km, as Bottke et al.'s model's do, which they put down to debris in orbit about Saturn from the impacts that reset the moons. A moon whose record starts after an impact it could not hold is now struck at once by that impact's debris (`cratering.debris_fragments`): fragments striking at 0.3 times its orbital speed (`cratering.debris_velocity`), their numbers falling with size at a cumulative slope of 2 below 2 km and 6 above (the shape of Mimas's excess, steep for the large and shallow for the small as Bottke et al. describe it), 3.5e-4 at or above 2 km per km² where the comets strike at 25 km/s (fitted on Mimas), and as the square of the comets' impact speed elsewhere (stated choice with no derivation behind it, standard ejecta scaling making the escaping mass of an impact of a given crater independent of its speed; without it Miranda, whose counts the comets alone match, would take 2.6 times its craters of 2 km). Mimas then keeps its counts within 13 percent from 3.5 to 50 km; Tethys within 1.15 times from 7 to 50 km; Dione within 1.4 from 5 to 50 km; Miranda within 1.45 at 2 km and 1.15 above; Rhea, whose record starts when its crust solidifies, takes no debris and keeps 0.6 of its counts at every size. The gardening diffusivity, which follows each body's present production of 1 km craters (F135), falls with the comets' shallow slope under 1 km: Enceladus's is an eighth of the Moon's, not 4.6 times it.
- **F137 The base relief from the largest crater a body holds.** The user saw on the small icy moons a fine, rough underlying relief, of the right kind as their height maps show but too fine and without large-scale variation, and asked whether one scale served every body. It was the primordial relief laid at formation under everything: noise from a third of the radius down to a ninety-sixth, its wavelengths fractions of the radius already, but its amplitude a quarter of the relief the crust's strength supports, which goes as the inverse of gravity: 148 km on Mimas against the Moon's 57, so that Mimas's relief spanned 36 km and its finest octave stood 1 km high over 2 km, five times as steep as the Moon's. Measured band by band (scratchpad band_spectrum.py: differences of blurs within 50 degrees of the equator, each map against its own total), the real Mimas, Moon, Mars and Ceres carry their relief most at twice their radius and beyond, and ours at a quarter to a half of it. The primordial relief now begins at the largest crater the body holds (its radius on ice, 1.5 radii on rock, the stamp's limit) and falls toward the loop's grid with the Hurst exponent of cratered terrain, its largest octave as rough as a cratered surface is at that crater's scale, ROUGHNESS_RMS_SHARE of its depth (F54), never more than a quarter of the strength's limit: 4.5 km on Mimas, 3.2 on the Moon, 1.4 on Mercury and Mars, 0.6 on Ganymede, whose shallow craters its low transition makes. Low gravity deepens the relief where it deepens the craters, and nowhere else. Mimas's bands now run 0.62, 0.53, 0.42, 0.31, 0.20, 0.11 and 0.08 from twice its radius to a thirty-second of it, against its height map's 0.57, 0.51, 0.42, 0.36, 0.27, 0.16 and 0.11, and the lumps of tens of km that crowded its ground are gone; Mars's bands match its map's within 0.1. The warm icy moons are not changed: their shells relax every wavelength over a few tens of km to nothing, where a shell floating on an ocean relaxes toward isostasy and keeps a share of it, the thicker floating higher.
- **F138 The thermal-orbital history of an icy moon.** A moon's eccentricity and its interior now evolve together, in one integration over its life in steps of half a million years (`orbital`), for every icy moon locked to its planet. The heat its tides make is the eccentricity's orbital energy, G M m e² / 2a, spent, and a free eccentricity decays by the moon's own dissipation in an e-folding time 2E / P: Mimas, with the porous core and the thin shell the stateless model gave it at its present eccentricity, 228 GW, would damp its eccentricity of 0.0196 in 0.4 Myr, against Meyer and Wisdom (2008)'s 325 Myr for a cold Mimas. A resonance that holds a moon now holds its forced eccentricity (`orbit.resonance_forced`: the Laplace resonance's Io, Europa and Ganymede, and Enceladus in its 2:1 resonance with Dione), with the cycle of F77. A past resonance (F50) supplies the energy the planet's tides feed it, stated as a heat (`orbit.resonance_heating`) or as the eccentricity the dynamics found, which supplies what that eccentricity gives a warm moon, as the dynamics assumed of it; the eccentricity rises until the moon dissipates as much, the equilibrium heating of Meyer and Wisdom (2007), which does not depend on the moon, so that a moon that dissipates little is pumped high and one that dissipates much is held low (a safety cap at 0.2, where Nakajima et al. 2019 find Enceladus's orbit crossing Tethys's). Otherwise the eccentricity is free: before a moon's first resonance it has none, whatever it formed with damped within a few Myr by its warm interior; after its last, its present value is the remnant of that resonance, which lets it go at the latest time that lets its eccentricity decay to the present value by the present, and the eccentricity never falls below the present value after (the remnant of an excitation the inputs do not state); a free moon with no resonance stated keeps its present eccentricity. The moon answers with its ice: its shell thickens and thins by the heat reaching its base and made in it against what it conducts, with the latent heat of the water it freezes and melts (a Stefan balance, stepped implicitly), from a warm start, the H2O layer liquid under the shell a warm moon's heat would hold or under a crust of 1 km (stated choice); frozen through, it melts once the heat reaching its base exceeds what the whole layer conducts with its base at the melting point. Its porous rocky interior dissipates (F68) only while its pores hold water: while an ocean touches it, freezing when the moon freezes through, and thawing again only once an ocean has lain over it for R² / κ, the time heat takes to diffuse through it (κ 1e-6 m²/s; Mimas's 72 km take 0.16 Gyr). The shell's own tides (F75) give the moon thresholds: a hard shell's heating and its conduction both go as the inverse of its thickness, so that above one eccentricity it thins away and below it freezes through (Mimas 0.026, Enceladus with a dry core 0.032, Miranda 0.043), and ice frozen to the rock, whose tide is an elastic sphere's, melts only at a higher one still (Mimas 0.066, within the two to three times its present value at which Walker and Rhoden find its melting began; Miranda 0.11). Mimas is a new reference body: frozen through by 0.3 Gyr and cold for four billion years; Meyer and Wisdom's Mimas-Dione 3:1 e²-Mimas resonance, 0.70 Gyr ago for a Saturn Q of 18,000 with an equilibrium heating of 1.9 GW, is 83 Myr ago at Saturn's dissipation as Lainey et al. 2017 measure it, supplying 16 GW; its eccentricity is pumped to 0.071 and its ice begins to melt 33 Myr ago, the shell overshooting to 11 km as the runaway is quenched, and the resonance lets it go some 5 Myr ago: it has today its eccentricity of 0.0196, a shell 28 km thick over an ocean 33 Myr old and a dry core, dissipating 6.6 GW, its eccentricity's e-folding time 15 Myr (Lainey et al. 2024: a shell 20 to 30 km thick over an ocean younger than 25 Myr). Miranda freezes through by 0.3 Gyr; its resonance, supplying the 42 GW that Ćuk et al. 2020's eccentricity of 0.0165 gives a warm, dissipating Miranda, pumps its eccentricity to 0.10 until its ice melts at 1.08 Ga, and holds it at 60 mW/m² under a 15 km shell, its eccentricity 0.044 while its core is dry, until it escapes at 0.85 Ga; frozen through again by 0.7 Ga. Enceladus, held from its warm start, keeps its 22.5 km shell, its wet core and its 29 GW. Ganymede's eccentricity rises toward 0.018 as its resonance's supply does and decays over 0.15 Gyr after, so that its lanes trail the resonance by 0.2 Gyr and the episode is placed 2.2 Gyr ago for its bright terrain of two. Europa's shell lags its cycle, 28.7 km today against 30.3; the thick oceans of Titan, Callisto and Triton are still freezing from the warm start (Triton's shell 260 km against 296).
- **F139 The elastic lid's flexural support.** The cold lid of an ice shell holds up the loads its flexure can carry and lets the longer ones settle onto the ductile ice below; F39 took the support as four times the lid's thickness, the same on every body. A thin plate's flexural wavelength is 2π (D / ρ g)^¼, its rigidity D = E Tₑ³ / 12 (1 − ν²) (Turcotte and Schubert 2002, section 3.17): it goes as Tₑ^¾ g^-¼, so that a small moon's lid holds loads many times its thickness. Mimas's 12 km lid over its young ocean holds Herschel up (its flexural wavelength 450 km, where four times the lid made 49 km and its ocean flattened Herschel within years), Enceladus's 12 km lid 370 km, Ganymede's 32 km lid 420 km; Ganymede's shell of 18 km at 4.5 Ga, its lid of 7 km, holds 130 km, so that its basins still relax to palimpsests early and its craters of 70 km keep their relief. The share of a load that settles rises smoothly from none at two thirds of the flexural wavelength to all at four thirds, about the thin plate's degree of compensation 1 / (1 + (λ_f / λ)⁴), which is half at the flexural wavelength; the plate's law itself leaves a short load a small share that never vanishes, and the relaxation's survivals, multiplied step after step, wore that share away (a 30 km crater on a shell with a 3 km lid lost half its relief over 24 steps where the plate holds 97 percent). Relaxation now wants lids of a kilometre or two, as Enceladus's relaxed craters do (Bland et al. 2012: 150 mW/m² or more), and the relaxation beside a renewed terrain under an episode's thinned shell (F66) takes less: a shell thinned to a fifth holds every crater under 75 km. A floating shell's isostatic share of a load, (ρ_w − ρ_i) / ρ_w, about 8 percent, is not added: it decays by the flow at the shell's base, a channel of the base's rheological thickness δ with 12 η (λ / 2π)² / (Δρ g δ³) for its time, within 1 to 80 Myr on shells with a basal viscosity of 1e14 Pa s (F137 had supposed it would keep their large scales); what keeps them is the lid's flexure.
- **F140 The shell's surface processes follow its history.** The focusing of tidal heating at a pole (F43, F53), the lanes (F44), the cracks (F76, F82) and the plumes (F57) took the shell's present state, or the peak of its history, as having held over the body's whole life, as it had for every body while each time's shell was the steady state of its heat. Mimas's ocean, 33 Myr old, gave it an Enceladus-like terrain at its pole, seven older ones the body had turned from it, lanes over a quarter of it, cracks of four billion years and plume fallout. A new expert node, `interior.ocean_age`, gives how long the shell has floated without freezing through, up to the present. The cracks form only within it, a young ocean's few against the retention age's; the focusing's past episodes only while the shell floated. The focusing thins the shell where its tides meet its conduction: a shell that conducts more than is made in it is freezing and thins by the share made, and the thinning grows over the shell's conductive time d² / κ since its ocean formed (both 1 for a body in a steady state). The lanes renew by the tidal heat the lid carries above the floor over the shell's life, the lesser of its tides and what it conducts, so that a shell melting under a lid still thick or freezing from a warm start renews nothing, against the same heat carried over LANE_RENEWAL_GYR, 0.56 Gyr, calibrated as ICE_RESURFACING_FLUX was on Ganymede's two thirds (0.69): a brief spike of heat renews little and a steady flux all it can. Mimas keeps no active polar terrain (its thinning 0.21 against the 0.3 that renews one), no past episodes, no plumes and a few cracks of its young ocean; its ocean's heat, 13 mW/m² carried for 33 Myr, three times the excess of Ganymede's episode, still renews a tenth of it in young lanes by this law, where Lainey et al. 2024 find its ocean too young to have marked its surface: the lanes want the strain of the lid, not its heat alone. Enceladus, Ganymede and Europa are as they were: their oceans are as old as they are and their shells in balance.
- **F141 The renewed ground keeps the base relief.** The floor a lane, a corona or a polar terrain laid was a plain low-pass of the relief at the terrain's smoothing length, so every old crater near that length survived as a soft dimple and all texture went with the craters: the user saw the renewed ground as a Gaussian blur, on Mimas's young lanes, on Enceladus's polar terrain and in Miranda's coronae, and asked whether the base noise field could be kept under the new terrain so that the craters smooth out and the micro-detail stays. A terrain levels the relief over what it stretches across, its own scale: a lane over its width (or the lanes' smoothing length, if more; it was the smoothing length alone, 10 km on Mimas against lanes 20 to 40 km wide, so that its 10 to 20 km craters stayed as dimples), a corona over half its outline's radius as before, a polar terrain over its zone's radius (it was a fifth of the body's; the thinned zone reworks everything inside it, and Enceladus's south polar terrain keeps no crater). What it levels is what impacts and older terrains added: under the floor the base relief (F137), the relief the body formed with, is kept below that length, in full at half the length and below and fading to nothing at it by the logarithm of the wavelength, so that two lanes of nearly the same width keep nearly the same relief and no seam runs between them. Below the spacing of the terrain's own fabric, its grooves, faults or folds, which overprint what is finer than themselves, the base relief is toned down to FINE_TEXTURE_SHARE of its amplitude, 0.4, over an octave (a stated choice, for the smoother look of new ice between its grooves, which the user asked for over the full relief's grain). The base relief is regenerated from the same field at whatever resolution draws it, down to two of its cells: the loop restores the octaves its grid holds when it lays the floor, as the bands the renewed cell gave up, which relax from then on like anything new; the finish adds those below the loop's cells, and all of them where it lays the floor itself, out to the lane's resolved edge. The floor's low-pass is read from the pyramid's levels blended by the logarithm of the length. Three tries went before: every octave below the smoothing length at full amplitude buried Mimas's grooves (108 m at 1.8 km under 340 m of relief); each lane's floor from its own pyramid level put sawtooth seams where overlapping lanes of a set, a level apart, crossed; and the base relief kept only below half the fabric's spacing left pixel grain over a blank mid-scale, grainier than the old ground in the shading and still blurred in the height map. Mimas's lanes are now crater-free ground with the base relief's undulation and a fine grain, their grooves faint on so thin a lid; Enceladus's polar terrain a plain at its depression with its folds, the ghosts of its 20 to 50 km craters gone; Miranda's coronae their faults over the relief their plains have.
- **F142 A lane's ends taper.** Every lane end that did not meet an older lane was cut off at a fault of its own across the lane, so that nearly every end was square (the taper for an end without a cut ran almost nowhere). An end that has run its length and meets no older lane now dies out in the old ground: its bounding faults converge and its grooves fade with their throw, the width closing as a linear wedge over three widths (a fault's displacement falls to its tip), so that the lanes pinch out in the long points Ganymede's do. An end that meets an older lane still stops at a cut along that lane's strike, square, as the junctions of Ganymede's lanes are. On Ganymede 0.4 of the ends stop against an older lane and the rest taper. The constants of the fault across the end, GROOVE_END_OBLIQUITY_DEG and the fault's wandering trace, are gone with it.
- **F143 A corona's belt keeps the old crust's tone.** A corona's ice was clean throughout, so Miranda's coronae stood bright against its plains, where Voyager's coronae are darker outside and bright within: the USGS map of Inverness splits them into an outer corona material and an inner one, and the user asked for the split, which the process already draws as the belt and the core (F133). The core is ice renewed from below and stays clean; the belt is the old crust broken into tilt blocks, which keeps the crust's own tone and shows clean ice only on its faults' scarps, as a fracture's walls do beyond the outline (0.6 of the clean share over the troughs). The loop's clean ice follows the same split. The belt is therefore as dark as the plains and no darker: a belt darker than the cratered terrain, as Inverness's and Arden's are, wants the dark component of the Uranian moons' ice, which is not among the materials yet (Miranda's plains show 0.58 against the measured 0.20 for the same reason, F126), and goes with the materials.
- **F144 The elastic lid of dry rock.** The isotherm that bounds a rock lid's elastic layer was 650 K, below Venus's 737 K surface, so Venus had no elastic lid at all. The literature's values are in Celsius: the oceanic lithosphere's elastic thickness follows the 600 °C isotherm (McKenzie, Jackson and Priestley 2005), Davaille et al. 2017 take 650 °C for Venus's dry rheology and Sandwell and Schubert 1992 750 °C; dry diabase creeps at 10^-17 /s under 100 MPa at 928 K (Mackwell, Zimmerman and Kohlstedt 1998: A 8 MPa^-4.7 /s, n 4.7, Q 485 kJ/mol). ELASTIC_ISOTHERM_ROCK is 923 K, 650 °C. Venus's elastic lid derives at 30 km against its measured global mean of 29 ± 6 km; Mars's goes from 98 to 160 km (its present north polar lid is over 300 km), Mercury's from 83 to 191, the Moon's from 156 to 267, Io's from 1.8 to 2.7. Mars's true polar wander (F104) moves by a degree or two: its rise outweighs the frozen bulge several times over.
- **F145 Coronae over a rock mantle's diapirs.** Venus's coronae were Phase 3's: 1.1e-12 per m², random places, sizes uniform in the logarithm, a dome or a sag by a coin's toss, fixed rims. They now come of the corona process F133 began, over a rock mantle. Besides its plumes a mantle sends up transient diapirs off its hot boundary layer (Johnson and Richards 2003), small plumes whose conduits feed their heads at 6.1e-9 kg/s per m² of the head's disc for 100 Myr (Smrekar and Stofan 1997: 0.30 Mg/s for an average corona 300 km across, their cases fed 50 to 430 Myr), as many as 15 percent of the heat the mantle convects into its lid since the lid last formed allows (their upwelling's share). Each is born where the heat comes up, fifteen times more often within a large upwelling's convection cell, as wide as the mantle is deep, than elsewhere, at a time drawn from that heat. Its head flattens under the lid, 65 km in radius log-normal by 0.5, as deep as it is wide, 100 to 300 K hot, a Gaussian of its radius (Dombard et al. 2007's thermals). It melts where it stands above the solidus at the base of the lid over it (Hirschmann 2000), out to where its excess falls to what the solidus asks of the mantle about it: that pocket is the corona, small pockets over heads that barely melt and wide ones over hot heads, as Dombard et al. argue. Where the mantle about it melts too, the melting is the region's, its floods' and plains', and no corona stands out of it: Mars's, Mercury's and the Moon's mantles through most of their histories, and Venus's for 200 Myr after its lid founders. A plume's hot head, half the radius of the dome it lifts, heats the mantle under it and thins the lid over it toward the stagnant lid the hotter mantle holds. The melt column grows as the square of how far the head rose above its solidus (Langmuir, Klein and Plank 1992: 10 percent per GPa), four fifths intruded. While its conduit feeds it the head thins the lid over it the same way, to 7 to 14 km at Venus's coronae (Smrekar et al. 2023: 11 ± 7 km). The corona stands where its loads bend that lid past 20 MPa at its surface in an annulus (Dombard et al. 2007: fault strengths 10 to 80 MPa), by the share it takes in faults of its own rather than as a swell over its flexural length, the rule of the icy coronae (F133). Venus gets some 750 coronae, 176 km across on average log-normal by 0.4 (Gülcher et al. 2025: 740 of 60 km and more; Glaze et al. 2002: 215 km on average, log-normal), a quarter within one and a half radii of its rises (Stofan et al. 1992: 21 percent at hot spots), a third still over a buoyant head (Gülcher et al. 2020: 37 of 125 large coronae with an outer rise and trench; Cascioli et al. 2025: 52 of 75 buoyant). Mars and the Moon get none, their mantles melting regionally or their lids too thick; Mercury three faint domes from its last half gigayear, where its mantle model still melts. The coronae take the lanes' renewal (F60, F61): the lava floods each core, which takes its time, and the belt keeps the old ground its faults break, whose blocks tilt only as far as the lid's bending strains it, a thousandth of the way to lock-up. The 68 percent of Venus's coronae along its chasmata wait for its rifts (M10), along which the lid thins; the novae are still a random share.
- **F146 A corona stands on its loads.** A corona's profile is the thin elastic lid's response to its loads, solved in the radius by finite differences (`flexure.rs`, agreeing with the Hankel transform's solution to 0.04 percent). What lasts: the melt's cumulates, 200 kg/m³ denser than the crust they displace, sink the pocket's floor, and the crust they push out thickens its edge (Dombard et al. 2007); half the erupted lava piles at the annulus's vents into the rim, a narrow load the lid holds up and bends down beside it into the moat (Sandwell and Schubert 1992), and the rest floods the floor up to the rim's crest, spilling over it as an apron (Stofan et al. 1991: edifices along the annulus, flows ponding). What goes with the head's buoyancy, held while its conduit feeds it and fading over the time its excess takes to diffuse across its half-thickness: its own lift, and the lid's lower part founding at the pocket's edge into a trench. Round the edge the rim's thickening varies by 0.7 of its mean and the drip comes and goes in arcs (Davaille et al. 2017: slabs in segments along the plume's rim; a third of the coronae with less than half an annulus), and the outline wanders by 0.15 of its radius. Domes, plateaus, rims round raised or sunken floors, moats, trenches and outer rises come of how wide each load is against the flexural length and how much buoyancy is left: a quarter of Venus's coronae have raised interiors and half rims round lows (Smrekar and Stofan 1997: a quarter domes and plateaus, nearly half with interior depressions), rims a few hundred metres to a kilometre. The finish draws the profile at the epoch, its time being the corona's event among the craters'.
- **F147 The lid's strain from the body's change of radius, and the faults it opens.** The faults that break a lid came only from a plume rise's bending and load (F105, F111), so the icy moons had none of their own, and Charon, whose northern terrain is broken by chasmata kilometres deep as its frozen ocean expanded (Beyer et al. 2017), had no preset. A body's change of radius now strains its lid the same way in every direction and through its whole thickness (`strain.rs`). An icy body grows as its ocean freezes, water taking 9 percent more room as ice I (the strain F82 answers over the last million years, Nimmo 2004), the ice frozen at each time the shell of its thermal history (F138); below where the pressure passes ice I's limit, 209.9 MPa (the triple point of ice I, ice III and the melt; Wagner, Saul and Pruss 1994), an ocean freezes as a denser ice and shrinks the body by 5 percent of what freezes (a stated choice between ice III's 2.6 percent at 350 MPa and ice II's 6 to 8; Hammond, Barr and Parmentier 2016 on Pluto), which only Pluto's ocean reaches; and every body shrinks as its interior cools (F16). The lid bears the strain as a membrane stretched alike every way, of stiffness E / (1 − ν), until its stress breaks the layer its faults cut: Anderson's normal faults at (1 − 1/K) and thrusts at (K − 1) times the pressure at the layer's base, K = (√(1 + μ²) + μ)², μ 0.69 in ice (Beeman et al. 1988) and 0.85 in rock (Byerlee 1978). Its faults take up the strain beyond and move the strain at which it would stand unstressed: a shell melted and refrozen to where it was breaks nothing new, and one melted beyond what it bears in compression takes up the shortening and stretches back by as much less what it bears in tension. The record starts as the craters' does, when the surface began to keep one or as long before the present as a renewed surface keeps its craters (Europa's 100 Myr). The extension the faults take up is spent on normal faults by the generator the rises' faults now share (`populate`): graben whose faults, dipping at 60° (Golombek 1979; Plescia 1991), meet at the base of the layer they cut, down to where the overburden reaches 11 MPa, at which the lid's stress no longer breaks it (a stated choice: Charon's Serenity Chasma, 40 to 50 km wide; it gives Tethys's Ithaca Chasma, 100 km wide, 87 km), within the lid's elastic part at the fault's time, above where the ice creeps rather than breaks, within its ice, and within a fifth of the body's radius, beyond which the lid is no thin shell (a stated choice: Ithaca, the widest graben known against its body, is 0.19 of its radius across), the layer varying by 0.2 in its logarithm from fault to fault (a stated choice). A fault slips per length as the rises' do times density over stiffness against rock's (Cowie and Scholz 1992: displacement grows with length as the stress over the stiffness), some four times rock's in ice (a stated choice between the stiffness alone, which would cut Charon's chasmata a tenth of their length deep against its at most 0.016, and the frictional stress at a depth, which would leave them shallower for their length than Mars's graben), within the depth its width allows (F111); a lid's faults stay graben, as they cannot cut below the lid's brittle layer. The faults run from four times as long as they reach down, as restricted faults lengthen rather than deepen once they reach their layer's base (Nicol et al. 1996; Soliva and Benedicto 2005: two to ten times as long as they are high; a stated choice within that), up to half a great circle. With no direction in the strain, each runs its own way until it meets an older fault at least as long, which took up the stress it would have run on, while a longer one cuts across; half run beside a fault drawn before, as sets of graben do (F112), so that they gather in belts. Each forms when the lid has stretched by as much as the faults before it took up, so the older are drawn first and stop the younger. Charon's lid stretched by 1.6 percent of its area since its record began 0.12 Gyr after it formed (Beyer et al. 2017: at least 1 percent), from 0.14 to 0.47 Gyr, before Vulcan Planitia's flooding some 4 Gyr ago: 58 faults, 220 km long at the median and up to 890 km, 46 km wide and 4.1 km deep at the median and up to 12 km (Serenity 40 to 50 km wide and more than 5 km deep; Mandjet at least 450 km long, 30 km wide and 5 to 7 km deep; Argo some 9 km deep).
- **F148 A wide trough across its turns, and an icy graben's ends.** A trough is drawn frame by frame along its trace, each frame a band across it, and a cell took the depth of the one frame nearest along the trace. Where the trace turns, at an en-echelon link most sharply (45°), the bands of the frames on either side spread apart on the outer side of the turn and left a wedge no band reached: a gap across the floor, nothing in a graben a few kilometres wide on a grid of ten, but bars and stripes across Charon's chasmata 46 km wide. Each frame's band now reaches out along the trace, for each metre off it, by the tangent of the turn it makes against the frames beside it, so that a turn's wedge is covered and a straight trace's bands stay as they were, and the frames that reach a cell share it, each weighted by how near the cell lies to its own line, so that the depth passes from one frame to the next without a seam. And each frame gathered every cell within the trough's reach of it, some 37,000 on Charon, to keep the few its band holds: it now walks only the strip its band reaches, with two cells to spare, so Charon's chasmata are drawn in a tenth of the time, bit for bit as before, and Miranda's wide graben in seconds rather than many minutes. A fault beside another (F112) took the host's points each shifted along its own normal in the tangent plane: some 200 km aside on Charon, round the 45° steps of the host's links its points jumped apart outside the turn, by up to hundreds of kilometres, and folded back inside it, so that a third of Charon's chasmata were broken. A fault beside another now follows the host's course smoothed over twice the distance between them, not each of its relay steps, its points moved along great circles and walked again at the host's spacing without what runs back on itself; and the spacings' log-normal is cut at two deviations (a stated choice), as a fault far beyond its neighbour's stress shadow does not run beside it.
- **F149 A graben's form.** The user saw the icy moons' new graben as broad soft troughs, too wide on Miranda and Charon, their edges beaded with circles and their floors one flat level, and asked for faults that look like cracks, physically placed and sized but drawn with whatever makes them convincing. Three things made the look. The lid's faults all cut to its brittle layer's base, log-normal about it by 0.2, so every graben was as wide as the widest can be; a lid's fault now cuts to a depth log-normal about half the deepest its faults reach, by 0.5 in the logarithm, and no deeper than the deepest (stated choices: Charon's chasmata run 10 to 50 km wide, Serenity the widest, and Pluto's fossae a few km to 20), so Charon's chasmata come out 24 km wide at the median and 44 at most, in 228 faults, 2 km deep at the median and up to 8, and Miranda's 20 km at the median. Each wall is a chain of straight pieces three widths long, each standing in or out of its neighbours by 0.07 of the width, the wall stepping from one to the next over a relay ramp 0.6 widths long, rough below that by 0.03 of the width at the width's wavelength, self-affine with the Hurst exponent of F112 down to the grid's cell, and swelling in and out by 0.08 of the width over eight widths, so that the two walls do not run parallel (stated choices: Pluto's and Charon's chasmata run straight for a few widths between jogs and widen and narrow along their length). The flat floor between tanh walls, cut anew, is gone: a graben's floor is the old ground let down between its faults, craters and all, as a ring graben's has been since F69 (a rift's or a canyon's floor is still cut anew, its ground foundered or filled), and only the fault faces are new ground, which shed their regolith as a crater's walls do (F98), so that the scarps stand bright on the icy moons. A graben's throw rose from each tip as a quarter circle, standing up at once at the tip, and its width narrowed over a whole width at each end, so a graben four widths long was a lens, and narrowed over 0.3 widths a pill with square ends; the throw now rises from each tip as a parabola whose slope at the tip is twice the throw per length F112 allows, the width falls toward the tips as the square root of the throw's share, to 0.3 of the width, and the walls close over 0.3 widths (stated choices: the fossae of Pluto and Tharsis taper to points), a rift's too. The wall's throw is the floor at the foot of its last face, found in a second pass, so the staircase meets the floor without a seam, and the faces are where the finish writes new ground and marks the steep layer.
- **F150 The lid's stress field.** A rise's faults read the stress of their own rise alone, in units of its crest's tension, and started wherever that passed a twentieth of it; the icy lids' faults took a strain the same every way and ran at random headings; and nothing joined one rise's faults to another's, so Venus had no rift network. The upwelling between neighbouring plumes: two plumes closer than four of their radii together sit on one upwelling, which pulls the lid apart across the great circle joining them by 0.8 of the lesser crest's tension, falling as a Gaussian over a rise radius to either side and fading beyond the rises' centres, growing as the lesser plume rises (a stated choice: Venus's Beta, Atla and Themis joined by Devana, Hecate and Parga Chasmata, Stofan et al. 1992; Mars's one plume has no limb). A neighbour whose course beside its host collapsed to a single step, a sixth of Venus's faults before, starts on its own instead. Venus: 34 rifts along its two limbs, 23,000 km of them, the longest 3,600 km, 26 km wide at the median and up to 105, with 7,700 graben in the belts about them; Mars's population as it was, its long canyons at the other end of its rise's axis by the draws, and a trough along the dichotomy's margin south-east of Tharsis, where the relief's stress turns the rise's far field along the contour.
- **F151 Coronae along the limbs.** Venus's coronae were drawn toward its plumes alone (F145), so none lay on the rifts that join them, where two thirds of the real ones lie (Stofan et al. 1992). The diapirs are now drawn along the upwelling limbs as toward the plumes, by the limb's pull. Two in five of Venus's coronae lie on its limbs and a third within 150 km of the limbs' fracture belts, a fourteenth within 150 km of a rift: short of the two thirds, because the rifts are a few lines in a belt a rise wide and because a diapir makes no corona where the mantle about it melts (F145), which the limbs do not heat (audit M25). Two more things were tried and dropped. Thinning the lid over the rifts, as measured, made it worse: a thinner lid puts the regional mantle above the solidus at its base, and the diapir's melt no longer stands out. Drawing the diapirs toward the rift zones as well, half the lid's flexural wavelength to either side of a rift, raised the share near rifts to a ninth in the derivation but moved the births into the hot ground about the rises in the loop, whose rifts the relief's stress had multiplied there, and the loop's count fell from some 730 to 167; the rifts no longer enter the corona process.
- **F152 The faults inside a wide trough.** A graben's terraces were drawn by window along its walls (F149, audit T1), a step fault here and there with a drawn share of the throw. They now come of a population: the hanging wall above a listric master fault breaks into blocks, as dominoes, each dropped along a fault synthetic to the master and tilted back toward it, so a wide trough's floor, between its bounding faults, breaks into blocks GRABEN_BLOCK_LAYERS of its faulted layer wide, the layer from its width by F147's rule (a stated choice of a quarter: Charon's Mandjet Chasma holds tilted blocks 5 to 10 km wide on a layer of 20 to 40 km, Beyer et al. 2017; Ithaca Chasma's terraced walls). A half-graben's blocks step down toward its master wall and a graben's toward its axis; the blocks together take GRABEN_INTERIOR_SHARE of the trough's throw, 0.3, the bounding faults the rest (a stated choice), split among the faults with each one's own scatter and capped as a graben's throw is by its width (F111). Each interior fault runs along the trough in pieces drawn from the faults' power law (F111), from two blocks long up to the trough's length, a gap of up to a block between, and is drawn as a graben of its block's width with its floor tilted the whole way, a new `tilt` on every trough that a rift's or a graben's half-graben floor keeps at RIFT_TILT, just after the trough it lies in, so it cuts the floor the trough let down, or the floor a rift cut, into a sawtooth of tilted blocks and steps the walls down in terraces. Only troughs whose blocks the grid resolves break, a block at least two cells wide: Charon's chasmata at 2048 pixels, Valles Marineris's troughs, not Pluto's fossae nor Venus's rifts at that size. The graben's own form keeps one face per wall; the terrace windows and their constants are gone.

The strain process now carries the faults the loop's grid resolves, two cells wide and more, as a rise's canyons are carried (F105): each is carved within the step it forms, its floor let down and its faces' ground renewed, and the finish subtracts the loop's coarse version and lays its own, as for a canyon; the narrow ones stay the finish's. A fault's window is its moment and a millionth of a gigayear, so the loop's progress through it is a step and not a division by nothing.
- **F154 The lid's compression.** The lid's stress field (F150) was read from its tensile side alone, by the normal faults and the rifts. A thrust reads it from the other: it strikes across the most compressive stress, and it forms where the lid is squeezed past what breaks its faulted layer in compression, Anderson's thrusts at (K − 1) times the pressure at FAULT_LAYER_DEPTH, K = (√(1 + μ²) + μ)² as for the normal faults (F147): 46 MPa on Mercury and Mars, 110 on Venus, 20 on the Moon. The body's shrinking squeezes a whole lid alike every way, its contraction (F16) over its radius times the lid's stiffness alike every way, E / (1 − ν); the field adds its compression in the lows and at the rises' feet and takes it away on the highs and the rises' crests. Only the field turns the squeeze, so a thrust's strike is the field's; where the field's differential stress is small against the scatter of the lid's own strength, a thrust follows the lid's weaknesses, its craters' rims and old faults, instead, and the field decides the strike as its differential stress over that and a tenth of the strength in compression (a stated choice). On the rocky bodies the field squeezes the lid by a few MPa on Mercury and the Moon, whose lids, 190 and 270 km thick, spread their relief's force thin, against the 313 MPa Mercury's shrinking would put on its lid; by ten to thirty-five on Venus; and on Mars by hundreds at Tharsis's foot, where the rise's bending squeezes a twelfth of the planet past the strength by itself. The loop reads the field's compression on a coarse grid of 64 cells to a cube face's edge, again as a rise grows by 0.02 of its height.

Each scarp starts where the overstress grew in its time bin, anywhere in its cell. Its longest length grows with the strain its place takes up by the present, by the scarps' length scaling refitted to Mercury's thousand-kilometre scarps at the 0.4 percent its thrusts take up beyond its lid's elastic strain (was 0.5 percent of its whole contraction). It strikes across the most compressive stress as far as the field decides it and its own way otherwise, its trace turning toward the field's strike by 0.35 a step where the field decides it (a stated choice, as the ridges follow their contours), bending its own way where it does not, scalloped by its lobes as before, and stopping where the lid is no longer squeezed past its strength. The scarps too short for the loop's grid are kept and drawn whole by the finish. A thrust whose trace runs mostly over lava breaks no scarp: wrinkle ridges are thrusts blind beneath layered plains that fold them (Schultz 2000; Mueller and Golombek 2004), so it is drawn as a ridge as high as the plains' ridges are, its slip taken up in the fold. Mercury: 7,200 scarps at 2048 pixels, 16 km long at the median and up to 840, against 2,350 carried before, and 2,600 thrusts folded into ridges on its smooth plains; its field is too weak to place or turn them, so they lie and run as they did, at random (audit M27). The Moon, whose contraction stays within its lid's elastic strain, has none, as before.
- **F156 The plains' ridges by the compression.** The plains' ridges followed the contours of the lava's thickness wherever the plains lay, with seeds weighted by its gradient and, around a rise, its load added to the thickness. They now stand where the lid squeezes the plains, read at each place at the time its ridges would stand: a whole lid's shrinking, or at least the plains' own drive, their subsidence as they cool and compact, which squeezes them RIDGE_DRIVE_SHARE past their strength (a stated choice, 0.03, against Venus's ridged plains, 93 percent of them below its mean radius, Bilotti and Suppe 1999), with the field's compression added and its tension taken away. Their seeds are weighted by that squeeze, saturating at the lid's strength, beyond which the ridges stand as densely as they can and the shortening goes into their slip, and by the thickness gradient as before; their length per area of squeezed plains is as before, and plains in tension take none. Each strikes across the field's compression as far as the field decides it and follows the thickness contours where it does not, meandering about that course in two harmonics of its wobble, which the course's pull no longer takes back, and stops where the plains end or are no longer squeezed. - **F157 The floods' loads on the lid, and the field through time.** The lid's stress field read the ground before the rises, low-passed over the flexural wavelength of the lid of the present, and held no load of the floods; the plains' ridges stood in for the floods' load with the lava's thickness, their seeds weighted by its gradient and their courses along its contours, and with a stated drive past the strength (F156, audit M26). The loop now reads the field at epochs, every 0.3 Gyr, at an episodic lid's renewal and at the end: the relief as the ground then stands, less the rises' domes the field holds itself, low-passed over the flexural wavelength of the lid as thick as it is then, which carries its potential energy's stress; and the stress of the lava laid since the last load, once 10 m of it lies anywhere (less is carried on), bending the lid of its time. Each load bends the lid as it was when the lava came and the stresses add, the lid that thickens beneath a load later bearing none of it. The lid is a shallow spherical shell over the mantle, D ∇⁴w + E h w / R² + ρ_m g w = q, of the rigidity of its elastic part then, under the lava's weight at the magma's density (F125); its membrane, with the Airy stress function ∇²Φ = −E h w / R, stiffens it and adds its own stress through its thickness to the bending stress at its top, the deflection's mean left out, which an incompressible interior lets no lid make (Turcotte et al. 1981). The Moon: 187 graben, a median 82 km from the maria's margins, 59 km long at the median and 3 km wide, formed between 4.05 and 3.2 Ga; Mercury 142 about its smooth plains; Mars some 300; Venus 22, its plains' lavas laid on a lid too thin for them to stretch it much.
