The thrusts beneath the plains' ridges take the same heights.
- **F160 Thrusts and ridges in systems.** The thrusts and the plains' ridges were drawn each on its own, so they lay as a scatter of separate lines, or, where the field turned them alike, as a whorl of arcs filling a basin, where Mercury's scarps gather in systems and the maria's few prominent ridges run in rings broken by gaps. They now gather as the lid's faults do (F112, F147). Half of them start beside one drawn before near where they start, the thrusts beside a thrust and the plains' ridges beside a ridge, the thrusts' ridges among them: a host chosen by length among those whose traces pass within a coarse cell or so of the start, so that a system grows where the lid is squeezed now, the newcomer running along a span of it about its point nearest the start, as long as its own length, to one side at 0.6 to 1.6 of the host's reach, the width over which it changes the ground (a stated choice: the Tharsis plateau's ridges some 30 km apart, Watters 1991; lobate scarps in arrays tens of kilometres apart), the plains' ridges only as far as the squeezed plains reach. The rest run their own way, across the field's compression as far as it decides them, and stop where they come within a quarter of their reaches of one drawn before at least as long, whose slip took up the squeeze they would have run on (a stated choice, as the lid's faults' arrest). The drawn are indexed on a coarse grid of cells 120 km wide. Mercury: 9,600 scarps against 6,800, shorter for their arrest, in clusters of parallel scarps; Mars's lava basin east of Tharsis: a set of long sinuous ridges among fainter ones where the ridges were even dashes; the Moon's central mare: groups of arcs with quiet ground between, where a whorl of arcs filled it.
- **F161 The shields' loads in the field.** The lid's field held the floods' lava as loads (F157) but not the shields', the greatest loads a lid bears. Each epoch's load now holds the height the shields have piled since the last as well, at the magma's density: the lid beneath and about a great shield is squeezed, and stretched in the flexural arch beyond, where graben ring the load (Comer, Solomon and Head 1985; McGovern and Solomon 1993; Alba Mons's own stress shaped the graben about it, Cailleau et al. 2003), and the thrusts, the plains' ridges and the loads' graben (F158) read it. The rises' domes stay out of the loads, held up from below by their plumes.
- **F162 The ground bent under its loads.** The loads stressed the lid but left the ground as it lay, so a shield stood on level plains and a filled basin kept the floor it was filled to. Each epoch's deflection is now laid into the ground, at the end of the step it falls in. Under the floods the ground sinks with the lid, and later floods fill what it opens; beneath a shield the edifice's lava fills the sag as far as it is thick, its surface staying as it built it, its height the magma's to raise and its root in the sag, while the ground about it sinks into a moat, deepest at its foot, and rises in a low bulge beyond (Olympus Mons's moat, some 2 km deep and partly filled by its aureole; McGovern and Solomon 1993). The edifice's root, the lava that fills its sag, is left out of the load (a stated choice: counted, it would deepen the sag again each epoch). The finish's floods see the subsidence after their step, as they see the shields' and the canyons' growth, so that their lava lies on the ground they found; the lava's own subsidence (LAVA_SUBSIDENCE) now stands for the pile's compaction alone. Mars's largest shield, 684 km across and 25 km high, stands in a moat 2.4 km below the ground at 2.5 of its radii, its summit 26.0 km where it was 27.2, and the region about Tharsis sinks 1.5 to 2 km under its lavas and shields, its lowest basin to −8.0 km; the Moon's central mare sinks some 1 to 1.5 km under its fill, while the lava in its craters, too narrow to bend the lid far, sinks less; Venus's resurfacing fill sinks the lid by up to 1.7 km; Mercury's deepest basin to −9.3 km from −7.2.
- **F163 The shields' lava at their feet.** A shield's flows stopped at its foot, its edifice all its lava, and the moat its load bends the ground into (F162) stood open. A shield's eruptions are F101's: fissures round its summit caldera, on its rift zone low on the flank and on its upper flanks, each a flow field whose area, its run-out squared, is its volume, spread evenly along its run as its breakouts are drawn by the lengths of the lobes laid (a stated choice). What their runs carry past the foot is 41 percent of a round shield's lava, nearly all of it from the rift zone, whose flows run 0.2 to 1.0 of a radius from vents low on the flank (Tharsis's young flows run 15 to 310 km, Peters et al. 2021, and the vents at the ends of Arsia and Ascraeus Montes' rift zones feed aprons onto the plains), and less where an elongated edifice's rift zone runs farther to its foot. Each step a shield sends that share of what its edifice grows by past its foot, in eruptions of a flood's size poured out at a shield's rates (SHIELD_EFFUSION_RATE), so that they come as compound flow fields of its own lava. Each starts at a point along a flow's run beyond the foot, drawn by the lava the flow carries there and never on another edifice standing higher than a flow is thick by then, whose flanks the lava would not climb (a neighbour that has hardly grown does not turn it, and where no point along the runs is kept the eruption starts at the foot at an azimuth of its own), and runs on down as a flood's lava does, ponding in the moat and spilling past its bulge; it loads the lid as the floods' lava does, so the moat sinks further under its fill (Olympus Mons's moat is thought mostly filled with lava and landslide deposits; Isherwood et al. 2013). Mars's shields send 5.4 million km³ past their feet, some two thirds of what their edifices hold: the largest shield's moat fills to 0.25 to 0.55 km below the ground at 2.5 of its radii, where it was 2.3 km deep, its lava hemmed in between it and its western neighbours standing to 7.1 km, and the smaller shields' moats stand 0.5 to 2.1 km deep, where they were 0.4 to 3.7. Venus's shields send 1.0 million km³ and fill their shallow moats by 0.1 to 0.4 km.
- **F164 The shields' melt is one budget.** A rise's shields took a share of the floods' eruptions (F108) that left the lava's budget whatever they did with it, while their edifices grew by a clock of their own (F120), and the two did not meet: Mars's shields took 6.6 × 10⁷ km³ of its floods' budget while their edifices held 8.3 × 10⁶ km³, and Venus's edifices and the lava past their feet held ten times what its shields took. A shield now grows from the lava its rise's eruptions bring it. Before the loop, step by step, each rise's started shields take the share of the step's extrusive budget that what the rise adds to the floods' vent weights, by its fertile mantle and its dome, takes of their whole, by the share they gather (F108, F119), read against the ground the loop begins on; they share it by their edifices' volumes, and the floods lose exactly what the shields take. A shield's edifice holds what its flows lay on it until it is full, while its flows carry their share past its foot (F163). A full edifice lifts no more, and the magma its rise still brings it intrudes beneath it (a choice: erupted as plains about the rise, Mars's would bury Tharsis under kilometres of lava; handed back to the floods, it would flood the dome and canyons as before F108). A full edifice's flanks go on being renewed by eruptions too small to count, at the pace its supply comes (a stated choice), its volcano's dike swarms come at shares of its growth (F116), and its calderas sink by the share of its height it built. Mars's budget is right by the measure: Greeley and Schneid (1991) put its volcanic deposits since the Early Hesperian at 6.45 × 10⁷ km³ and its Amazonian rate at 0.012 to 0.014 km³/yr, where the model's late rate is 0.012 to 0.016. Mars's shields take 7.1 × 10⁷ km³: their edifices fill by 1.6 to 1.9 Gyr, the largest by 2.9 Ga (Olympus Mons's main stage had ended by 2.54 Ga, Isherwood et al. 2013), holding 8.3 × 10⁶ km³ with 5.6 × 10⁶ km³ past their feet, and 5.7 × 10⁷ km³ intrudes beneath them. Venus's shields take 3.4 × 10⁵ km³ of its small budget after the overturn and stand 6 to 15 percent built, 0.4 to 1.6 km high (audit M29).
- **F165 The plumes' melt at their rises, and a volcano's weight gathering its rise's lava.** The mantle's melt was reckoned with its plumes' excess heat over the whole body (F123), and an episodic lid kept a stated 2 percent of it after its overturn, "the rest having gone into the event". The ambient mantle now makes the share of the melt its own gate keeps, and the plumes the rest only under their heads, the area their rises' provinces cover: the floods' budget is the ambient mantle's melt anywhere and the plumes' over their heads, and the vents weigh the ambient mantle's provinces by its share. Once the mantle under the lid has cooled below its solidus there, only the plumes melt, and their lava comes out at the rises. Venus's ambient mantle stops melting 0.45 Ga ago, as its overturn's surge ends; Mars's tapers from 1 Ga to nothing at the present, and its eruptions since 1.3 Ga come to 1.19 × 10⁷ km³ where Greeley and Schneid (1991) put its Middle and Late Amazonian deposits at 1.18 × 10⁷ km³ (they had been 1.95 × 10⁷). On an episodic lid the melt that comes while the ambient mantle still melts after the overturn is the overturn's own resurfacing, which the event lays; the floods after it are the plumes'. A rise's started shields share its lava by the area each gathers dikes from: its own vents' area and the area over which its weight's stress, its weight over that area, outweighs the regional stress, the rise's, at the lid's yield at its peak and falling as its growth wanes (F119; a load turns rising dikes toward it from where its stress outweighs the regional stress, Muller, Ito and Martel 2001). As the rise's growth ends the shields gather by their weights alone: the leading volcano keeps its lead, and one that starts as the rise stops never grows and sends no dikes. Venus's lava after its overturn, 1.05 × 10⁶ km³, all comes out at its rises, and each rise's leading volcano takes most of it: 5.1, 4.6 and 2.8 km high, 296 to 328 km across, as Maat Mons stands 4.9 km over its plains, 395 km across, with flanks of 1.5 to 2 degrees; their neighbours stand 0.3 to 0.9 km or never grew. Mars's largest shield fills by 3.0 Ga, the next two by 2.1 and 1.3 Ga, and the two smallest stand half and a third built.
- **F166 An edifice's root weighs on the lid.** The lava that fills a shield's sag was left out of its load (F162): counted epoch by epoch it would have deepened the sag again each time. It is now counted where it lies. Beneath an edifice the lid sinks into the edifice's own lava, which fills the sag as far as the edifice is thick, so that the mantle pushes back there by the difference between the rock's density and the lava's, not by the rock's whole, as a load sinking into its own infill does (Turcotte and Schubert, Geodynamics): the plate is solved with its restoring force cell by cell, the share of the sag the edifice fills read from the deflection it fills and then once more, and a floor of 100 kg/m³ (ROOT_MIN_CONTRAST, a stated choice) keeps a lava as dense as the rock afloat. The lid's strength holds the root within a few times the sag of the edifice alone: a shield 600 km across and 20 km high sinks 16.4 km beneath its summit on a Mars lid 70 km thick, where it sank 10.0 km, 1.25 times as far on a lid 30 km thick and 1.16 times on Venus's 25 km. Mars's largest shield now stands in a moat some 3 km below the ground at 2.5 of its radii, the next in 4 km, the lava past their feet filling part of them, their summits where they built them; Venus's leading volcanoes' surroundings sink some 0.2 km. The magma a full edifice cannot lift (F164) is not a load beneath it: Mars's largest shield's would stand as a column some 115 km thick over its footprint, thicker than the lid, so it spreads beneath the rise as the underplating of its crust, lighter than the mantle it replaces, which the rise's dome holds (a stated reading).
- **F167 A crater's elongation follows its cratering efficiency.** A cavity came out elliptical below an angle fixed for every crater on every body, the one below which 5 percent of impacts strike (about 12°; Bottke et al. 2000), so the largest basins were as round as the small craters. A cavity comes out elliptical, longer than 1.1 times its width, below a threshold angle that rises as the cratering efficiency falls, the diameter of the crater a vertical impact makes over the impactor's: laboratory impacts in sand, aluminium and lead and simulations in the strength and gravity regimes fall on one trend (Collins et al. 2011, Figure 1), whose line is 90° times the efficiency to the power of −0.73 (ELLIPTICAL_THRESHOLD_EXPONENT, digitised from the figure, within 0.2° from efficiencies of 1 to 100). The efficiency is the transient cavity over the impactor, from the body's own crater scaling (F135) at its mean impact speed for every population that strikes it (a stated choice), tabulated by the crater's diameter: the larger the crater, the lower its efficiency in the gravity regime and the steeper its threshold. Below the threshold the elongation grows as simulations of oblique impacts on a flat target show, 1.1 at the threshold, 1.25 at three quarters of it, 1.7 at half and 1.01 at one and a half times (Collins, Miljković and Davison 2013), up to 1.8 as before. Mars's craters of 10 km come out elliptical in 5.8 percent of impacts and those of 30 km in 8.3 (some 6.5 percent are observed from 10 to 40 km; Collins et al. 2011), its basins of 2,500 km in 35 percent (their model, near 30); the Moon's craters of 10 km in 3.5 percent. The largest basins are elongated as Borealis (10,650 by 8,520 km; Andrews-Hanna, Zuber and Banerdt 2008), Hellas, Utopia and South Pole–Aitken are (aspect ratios above 1.2; Andrews-Hanna and Zuber 2010). Planetary curvature, proposed to elongate them further, suppresses elongation in simulations instead (Collins, Miljković and Davison 2013) and is left out. The node cratering.oblique_fraction is retired.
- **F168 The dichotomy is an outcome.** The primordial crust carried a hemispheric contrast, half an amplitude times tanh(3 cos θ) about a random axis, the amplitude by the regime's name (20 km of crust on every stagnant lid): a smooth slope over thousands of kilometres that the basins hid (T5). The crust now starts without it, and a dichotomy comes of the impacts the body takes. A rocky body holds a basin as wide as a hemisphere: Mars kept the Borealis basin, 10,650 by 8,520 km (Andrews-Hanna, Zuber and Banerdt 2008), its long axis half the planet's circumference, where F135 held a rocky body to its own diameter (Vesta's Rheasilvia, 1.93 radii, a small body's survivor rather than a planet's limit), so Mars's record starts 0.47 Gyr after its formation rather than 0.57, and it takes about twice the craters it took (21 basins over 1,000 km where it took 11; Frey 2008 counted some 20, buried ones among them); the Moon's, Mercury's and Venus's records start when their crusts solidify, as before. The impacts wider than the stamp draws, from 1.5 body radii up to a hemisphere, are mega-impacts, which take a form of their own. The basin excavates the crust inside its elliptical rim (F167) down to the crust its melt sheet makes, half the body's starting crust (MEGA_FLOOR_CRUST_SHARE, a stated choice between Mars's northern lowlands, some 32 km against the southern highlands' 58, Neumann et al. 2004, and the thinner crust of South Pole–Aitken's floor, Wieczorek et al. 2013), the excavation giving way to the rim over the outer twentieth of its radius (MEGA_WALL_SHARE, a stated choice: a boundary scarp for the surface processes to wear). The excavated crust lands beyond the rim, thinning with distance as a basin's ejecta does (BASIN_EJECTA_EXPONENT), its volume kept, and thickens the crust there. The ground stands where the crust's buoyancy in the mantle holds it (Airy, as the primordial crust's does): the floor, where the melt ponded level, at the height its new crust floats to against the body's mean column, whatever stood there before, and the ground beyond raised by the ejecta's crust, the older relief under the ejecta buried by its thickness as a crater's ejecta buries it, and the continuous ejecta renews the surface within two radii, as a crater's does. The thin crust the basin leaves draws the later floods to it (F36), which fill its lowland. The crust a stamped basin thins under it, by excavation and the mantle's rise, was lost, so that the bombardment drained the crust it struck, Mars's from a mean of 40 km to 28 by its mega-impact; it now lands beyond the basin's rim, thinning outward as the basin's ejecta does, the crust kept and thickest in an annulus about the basin, as the lunar basins' are (Wieczorek et al. 2013). On the Mars preset (seed 1048) the basin's floor stands some 2.2 km below the datum and the ground beyond its rim some 2.2 km above it, 4.4 km apart (the Martian dichotomy is some 5.5 km), the floods having raised the floor by some 0.4 km since. Over 200 seeds Mars takes 1.34 mega-impacts on average, at least one on 74 percent of them and one wider than 2.5 radii, a Borealis, on 22 percent; the Moon 0.58, on 42 percent (South Pole–Aitken, some 2,500 km, falls just short of one), Mercury 0.46, on 35 percent; Venus and the Earth few, the icy moons none, their hold below the stamp's limit. The nodes tectonics.dichotomy_mode and tectonics.dichotomy_amplitude are retired.
- **F169 An ejecta deposit levels only the hollows it can fill.** A crater's ejecta buried the older relief under them by their thickness against its height above the mean of the ring about the crater the point lay on, and a mega-impact's (F168) against the ground low-passed over its basin's radius: around the largest basins the reference was the mean of thousands of kilometres, so that a deposit kilometres thick buried swells as wide as a hemisphere, and the user saw a Moon struck by one (master seed 6342) left flat but for its basin, where the primordial relief (F137) had been believable ground. A deposit fills the hollows it can and drapes the rest: it levels the ground over the width of the fresh crater as deep as it is thick, the largest hollow it can fill, from the body's own crater scaling, but no farther than it can spread, as far as a landslide's debris runs out for its fall (LANDSLIDE_REACH's longer end, 14 times, F106). On the Moon a deposit 10 m thick levels over some 50 m, 100 m over 500 m, 1 km over 5 km, 3 km over 32 km and 10 km over 140 km. A crater's rings split into sectors about that long, whose means are the reference; a mega-impact's reference is the ground low-passed over that width, cell by cell. The longer relief stands under the ejecta as it stood, draped. The Moon of master seed 6342, struck by a mega-impact 4,302 km across, keeps its far side's swells and lowlands under the ejecta, and the belt beyond its basin's rim, which the old reference had partly buried as relief, stands some 6 km high, as the highlands about South Pole–Aitken reach 6 to 8 km; Mars's highlands along its dichotomy stand higher and roll, the heights of its lowland gathering about −1.8 km and its highlands spreading to some 5 km.
- **F170 A rocky lid's lower crust flows where it is warm.** An ice shell's relief relaxed (F39) and a rocky lid's never did. Where the base of a rocky crust is warm its lower crust flows, a channel at the crust's base as thick as the depth over which its viscosity falls by e along the crust's conductive profile, δ = R T² / (Q dT/dz), beneath the crust it holds rigid; the pressure of the relief above drives it, moving the crust from under the high ground to under the low, so that, the crust kept compensated, the relief diffuses with κ = (Δρ/ρₘ) ρ g δ³ / η and a wavelength decays as (λ/2π)² / κ, the longest lasting (McKenzie, Nimmo and others 2000; Nimmo and Stevenson 2001), where the elastic lid does not hold it up (F139's support, with rock's modulus). The lower crust creeps as dry diabase (Mackwell, Zimmerman and Kohlstedt 1998: n 4.7, Q 485 kJ/mol, A 8 MPa⁻ⁿ s⁻¹), its viscosity read at the stress a kilometre of relief drives and never below 10¹⁸ Pa s (stated choices); the crust's base stands on the lid's conductive profile from the surface to the mantle at the lithosphere's base, under the crust's tenth thickest, whose base is the warmest (a stated choice; the relaxation is the body's, band by band, not the cell's). The relief relaxes toward the domes the plumes hold up, which are not the crust's. A stagnant rocky lid relaxes (a stated scope): a mobile lid's crust is its plates' (Phase 6); Io's heat pipe is not the conductive lid its thermal history describes; and an episodic lid's highlands, Venus's tesserae, stand on the primordial relief rather than on a crust their tectonics thickened, which its crust, its base above 1,600 K through its first billion years, relaxes away, leaving Venus flat but for its volcanoes (audit M31). The model's thermal histories keep the base of Mars's, the Moon's and Mercury's crusts below 1,200 K, where diabase holds its relief for the age of the solar system, as Nimmo and Stevenson found for a Martian crust thinner than some 100 km, so that their dichotomies and basins stand as they formed and their maps are unchanged; a body whose crust is thicker or whose interior is hotter relaxes: under a crust 100 km thick, Mars's crust's base would stand at 1,600 K at 0.1 Gyr, and in its first half billion years its relief 1,000 km long would relax away and two thirds of that 3,000 km long, while its elastic lid held up that 300 km long.
- **F171 The floods fill the low ground outside the provinces too.** A flood's vent was drawn by the crust's thinness and the ground's lowness (F36) times the magmatic provinces' weight, a Gaussian about each province over a background of 0.03 of its centre: the provinces outweighed everything outside them some thirty times, so that on Mars, whose northern lowland is its lowest ground on its thinnest crust and draws five to thirteen times the vents per area that its highlands do, the floods spread as evenly as the provinces lay, 643 m of lava over the lowland against 659 m over the highlands, of which some 270 m Hesperian. Mars's Early Hesperian plains filled most of its northern lowlands to some 800 to 1,000 m, and its highland basins too, a flood-basalt phase over the low ground rather than within provinces (Head, Kreslavsky and Pratt 2002). A province's centre now stands four times the rest (MAGMA_PROVINCE_FLOOR 0.25, fitted on Mars): the lowland holds some 900 m of lava against 600 m on the highlands. The Moon's lava does not gather in its province at any weight, its basins' thin crust deciding where it lies (audit M33). The lowland's craters keep their rims under it, the lava pooling on their floors: craters of 50 to 200 km stand 2 to 3 km deep, and the lowland's plains bury their own to ghosts with the sediments of Phase 5 and the young surface they leave.
- **F172 An edifice its crust cannot hold sheds its flanks.** A shield's slope (SHIELD_SLOPE_DEG) would build it as high as its radius allows, and the relief its crust's strength holds (interior.max_relief, F15) caps it: the edifice that reaches that cap stood at it untouched, as its smaller neighbours do below it. Olympus Mons is ringed by a basal scarp 6 to 10 km high and by its aureole, lobes of landslide deposits, Lycus Sulci the largest, reaching some 700 km beyond its edge and more than 2 km thick where two overlap (DLR and ESA 2023). An edifice past what its crust holds now sheds its flanks once it has filled to the cap: the lava its supply would have stacked above the cap spreads them instead, as it comes, as much of its toe as that lava is pushed out beyond its foot and breaks away, and the edifice ends in a scarp where the toe beyond it holds that much, its wall at 30° (SCARP_WALL_SLOPE_DEG) and its reach varying round the edifice by 6 percent of itself (SCARP_WARP; stated choices). The toe and the lava together, twice the spread lava, run out beyond the scarp as its aureole, in four to seven lobes sharing the circle (AUREOLE_LOBES), each as far as a rock avalanche of its volume runs for the scarp's height (Scheidegger 1973's trend as it is quoted, log₁₀(H/L) = −0.15666 log₁₀ V + 0.62419 with V in m³, which gives the canyons' landslides of some 400 km³ a reach of 16 beside F106's 7 to 14), widening by LANDSLIDE_SPREAD to its toe as a landslide's lobe does, its surface ridged across its run some 12 km apart, the ridges' crests wandering by up to a spacing and their height coming and going over a few, as Lycus Sulci's are curved, broken and uneven (AUREOLE_RIDGE_SPACING, AUREOLE_RIDGE_SHARE, AUREOLE_RIDGE_WARP; stated choices), the ridges drawn by the finish. The edifice fills first and its flanks fail as the lava after that comes, the toe beyond the scarp breaking away and the aureole spreading with it, the supply covering the aureole with the edifice (F164); the aureole weighs on the lid in the moat with the lava (F162). An edifice its supply never fills never fails: Venus's three largest shields would build 12.9 to 14.3 km against the 10.5 km its crust holds, but its supply builds them only part of the way (F165), and none fails. Mars's largest shield, 626 km across and 25 km high where its slope would build 27.4 km, ends in a scarp at 0.81 of its reach, 4.5 to 9.9 km high round it, and lays seven lobes that run out 120 to 350 km, shorter than Olympus Mons's longest, a smaller share each running less far; its smaller neighbours stand below the cap and keep their toes. On the map the largest shield is ringed by its cliff, and its aureole's corrugated lobes lie in its moat and beyond.
- **F173 The mantle's convection holds the ground up.** A rocky mantle's history (F121) was one temperature, one lid and one flux, with nothing across the body: its rises stood where they were drawn, and the ground beyond them where the primordial relief (F137) and the impacts left it, though some nine tenths of Venus's relief is held up by its mantle's flow (Simons, Solomon and Hager 1997), its rises over the upwellings and its lowlands over the downwellings. Beneath a stagnant or an episodic lid the mantle now convects in the layer from the stiff lid's base to the core, in cells as wide, an upwelling to the next, as 2√2 times the layer's depth (CONVECTION_CELL_WAVELENGTH, a stated choice: the wavelength at which convection between free-slip boundaries sets in, Chandrasekhar 1961), its power spread over the degrees about theirs (CONVECTION_SPECTRUM_WIDTH, a stated choice): a few large cells in a thick mantle and many small ones in a thin one, as King (2008) found Mercury's thin mantle convecting in many narrow rolls where Venus's, the Earth's and Mars's rise in a few plumes. The ground the flow holds up, raised over the upwellings and drawn down over the downwellings, has a root mean square of 2.2 times α ΔT_rh δ_rh, the convecting sublayer's thermal contraction over its thickness, its temperature drop LID_BASE_SCALES rheological scales and its thickness k ΔT_rh / q from the mantle's own stagnant-lid flux (CONVECTIVE_TOPOGRAPHY: the mean over the eleven time-dependent stagnant-lid simulations of Guimond, Rudge and Shorttle 2022, 1.5 to 3.2 across viscosity contrasts of 10⁶ to 10⁹, whose sublayers are k ΔT_rh / q thick within 8 percent; their own fit in the interior Rayleigh number and the viscosity contrast runs away beyond the range it was fitted on, where this form holds); an elastic lid holds it flatter at the wavelengths it bends over, a wavelength's deflection 1 / (1 + D k⁴ / ρ g) of the mantle's own. A layer convects once it is several sublayers deep (CONVECTION_ONSET, a stated choice). The pattern renews itself in the time the flux takes to carry off the heat the sublayer holds, ρ c_p ΔT_rh δ_rh / q, and at each overturn of an episodic lid, one state turning into the next along a quarter circle so that it keeps its strength. The pattern is chaotic, so it is drawn from the body's seed, as the craters' places are; its scale, its strength and how fast it changes are the mantle's. The loop lifts and lowers the ground as the pattern stands at each step's end, before the step's craters and floods, so that the plains pond in the lows of their time and are warped as the pattern moves on; the lower crust relaxes toward it rather than away (F170), and the lid bears none of it, as it bears none of the rises' domes (F150): the relief the field reads leaves it out, as the overturn's tesserae do. A body's plumes rise where its mantle wells up most strongly as they start, the strongest first, each apart from those before, where they were drawn anywhere, and the pattern turns with the body as its rises turn it (F104). The upwelling limbs between plumes (F150), which pull the lid apart across them, ran along the great circles between plumes nearer than four of their radii together; they now follow the ridges of the pattern the plumes rose from, each joining two plumes whose ridge stands above the mantle's mean all the way between them (UPWELLING_LIMB_SADDLE, a stated choice), along the way that keeps highest (UPWELLING_LIMB_RIDGE, a stated choice), however long, so that the plumes a downwelling parts stay apart, and the rifts they open and the coronae they draw (F151) follow the mantle's flow. Venus's cells are of degree 4.3 now, its pattern renewed 287 times, the ground it holds up 302 m at its root mean square and from −1,350 to 890 m; Mars's of degree 2.5, 396 m and −930 to 1,070 m; the Moon's, whose mantle reaches its centre, of degree 1, 641 m and about a kilometre each way; Mercury's of degree 14, 658 m before its thick lid holds it flatter and 144 m after, about half a kilometre each way. On the maps Venus's rises stand at its upwellings and a lowland 2 km deep lies over a downwelling, two of its plumes joined by a ridge 14,800 km long, arching north between them where the great circle is 9,100 km, its rifts along it, the longest some 1,900 km, and the third plume parted from both by downwellings; Mars's rise straddles its dichotomy's boundary at the western end of its highlands; Mercury and the Moon roll gently over their mantles' cells.
- **F174 The melt is one budget, and a rise is a pile as well as a dome.** A rocky body's melt erupted by the share its crust's buoyancy left (F125, an eighth at most), and the rest, seven parts in eight or more, was lost: on Mars 1.1 × 10⁹ km³ of its 1.25 × 10⁹, as were the 4 × 10⁷ km³ its full edifices could not lift (F164). Tharsis's long-wavelength gravity and Mars's shape are explained by a load of some 3 × 10⁸ km³ of magma (Phillips et al. 2001), most of it in place before about 3.7 Ga (Hynek et al. 2011), where the model's rise was a dome its plume held up and no volume of its own. The melt that stays below now intrudes the crust beneath where it rose from, as the vents' sources are without the ground the eruptions find their way through: the provinces the ambient mantle melts in by the share of the melt it makes, and the plumes' heads the rest (F165), reckoned as the eruptions' budget is, over the same times, so that what erupts and what stays below make the melt; and the magma a full edifice could not lift intrudes beneath its rise. The finish sees the intrusions at the epochs the loop laid them, as it sees the ground's sinking (F162). On Mars 1.16 × 10⁹ km³ intrudes, 3.2 × 10⁸ km³ of it beneath its rise, as much as Tharsis's load, the most in its first billion years; the rise's flanks stand some 2 km higher, at some 7 km, 22 km intruding beneath its centre against 4 km where the least does, the old basin it stands over raised some 2.5 km to 2 to 3 km and its largest shield 27.3 km on the new ground. Venus, whose melt after its overturn is small, changes little; the Moon and Mercury, whose crusts held most of their melt down, the Moon's light crust early and Mercury's shrinking lid after its first billion and a half years, swell over their provinces by a kilometre or two, the Moon's raising the lowland its province lies in, where its maria do not gather (audit M33). The intrusions raise the ground over a province about evenly, so that the basin keeps its depth below its rim; only lava fills a hollow, and the vents favoured low ground against the body's median (F36), so that a hollow on a raised rise drew few of the floods where Tharsis's lavas erupted high on theirs (F176). The rise's dome still stands 0.28 of the relief its crust holds, the share Tharsis's whole height was fitted to before it had a pile (a calibration on one body to revisit: the dome from its plume head's buoyancy).

The level is now the sectors' means interpolated round each ring between the sectors' middles and across the rings between theirs, so that it changes smoothly round and away from the crater, the relief longer than a sector still draped rather than buried. On a plain tilted 3 m per km a crater 600 km across leaves the ground beneath its thick deposit near the rim stepping no more than 13 m between points 3 to 4 km apart round it, where the sectors' edges stood as steps of 28 m; beneath the deposits of Mars's and the Moon's basins, on relief kilometres high, the terraces are gone.

The load's mean now leaves it before the plate is solved, the deflection's mean and first degree leave the membrane, and what a load laid evenly raises is the body's growth, its mean radius, the heights' datum, with it, so that the ground keeps its height above the mean radius; the finish sees that with the sinking (F162).
