- **F190 The tesserae's folds and graben as lines.** The fabric was a sum of wave packets, each with its coarse cell's strain and a drawn phase: at a global view a blur, the user's frosted glass, where the noise fabric before it had a turtleshell of ridged cells. Varying the packets by the old noise could not make cells, which need rims standing above their interiors, and the noise may not add relief of its own (T1). Now each fold's crest and each graben is a line along the strain's strike (`stages/tessera.rs`), a set's lines as far apart as their spacing, traced as evenly spaced streamlines (Jobard and Lefer 1997) from coarse cells' centres and from seeds a spacing to either side of each line drawn: a line runs on while the strain keeps its sense, no other line of its set comes within half a spacing, the strike does not turn sharply where the strain is alike every way, and its own length, drawn between 3 and 15 spacings (TESSERA_LINE_LENGTH, a stated choice: periclinal folds three times their spacing and more, Ovda's long folds up to 400 km), lets it, its relief plunging out over a spacing at its ends. A fold is a narrow crest over broad troughs to half a spacing, fading to the mean by three quarters; a graben a flat floor a third of its spacing wide (TESSERA_GRABEN_FLOOR) between walls a cell wide, flat blocks between. The spacing scatters by a factor of two to the 0.45 either way and the relief by half (a fold train's spacing scatters by a third or so, its amplitudes two to three times). A set is drawn where its spacing spans three cells, fully from five (TESSERA_MIN_CELLS, a stated choice: fewer cells to a spacing draw a hatch of cells, the user's fine seashell, which the artistic pass's detail stands for); on a 4,096-pixel Venus the lid's folds and graben and the collapse's brittle folds show, the drag's brittle folds and the ribbons on finer grids. The folds stand as curving ridge belts, en echelon, crossing as cells where two sets meet; each layer's relief still saturates at its strength (F189). The tessera share is 7.9 percent, the highest 7.4 km, and the event's pass takes 31 s at 4,096 pixels.
- **F191 The tesserae's graben are the model's graben, and the lid's its rifts.** The tesserae's graben stood at most 0.27 km deep, the weight of their relief against the lid's tension at the fault layer's depth, a bound the tesserae alone had; the tiles the collapse's crossing graben bound showed faintly. A yield envelope, the lid's friction through its thickness, would have deepened them, but it was set aside in the ninety-fourth milestone, its 150 to 380 MPa breaking no lid (F178), and here it would break Venus's young lid nowhere under the drag, emptying the strain record. The tesserae's graben now follow the model's graben (F111, F112): their faults, dipping at GRABEN_DIP_DEG, meet at the base of the brittle layer they neck, which sets their width, about a third of their spacing (replacing the stated floor share), and they sink with the stretching no deeper than that layer, a fifth of their width (GRABEN_DEPTH_PER_WIDTH) or the throw their length allows (SEGMENT_THROW_PER_LENGTH); the elastic lid necked whole cuts below any faulted layer and opens rifts (F38): as deep as their heave, 1.2 of their depth (RIFT_HEAVE_PER_DEPTH), up to 1.5 thousandths of their length (RIFT_DEPTH_PER_LENGTH), thirty depths wide (RIFT_WIDTH_PER_DEPTH) and no wider than half their spacing. By the graben's rules alone the lid's troughs, 22 km wide, sank 4 km, a grid of gashes across the plateaus; as rifts they stand some 0.3 to 1.5 km, as the troughs across Venus's tesserae do. The graben and rifts are bounded by their faults, not by the folds' strength (F189). On a 4,096-pixel Venus the collapse's rifts, some 70 km apart, cross the plateaus' crests in tiles; the tessera share is 7.8 percent and the event's pass takes 30 s.
- **F192 The tesserae's graben take the model's graben form, and the lid rifts once.** The collapse's crossing rifts read as a grid ending abruptly against the fold belts. Four changes, each the model's own rule: (1) relief approaches its bound smoothly, bound x (1 - exp(-raw/bound)), so a trough or fold grows in where the strain does rather than standing at its bound wherever the strain passes it; (2) the greater stretching breaks the lid first and a later fault of the lesser ends where it meets an earlier one, as a lane's do (F61); (3) a line follows the strain's strike only as far as the strain decides it, its differential against THRUST_STRIKE_SCATTER of its mean, starting along a direction of its own where it does not and bending up to SCARP_TURN_DEG, as a thrust does (F154, F155) - the collapse's strain is decided nearly everywhere, its differential far past its mean, so this changes little on Venus; (4) the elastic lid necks whole across its greatest stretching alone, its rifts taking the heave, the lesser stretching left to the brittle layer's graben. And the graben have the model's graben form (F111, F112): a parallel set stands GRABEN_SPACING_LAYERS of its layer apart wall to wall, log-normal with GRABEN_SPACING_SPREAD, no less than a quarter of it (replacing the necking spacing for graben); each graben is a chain of segments SEGMENT_LENGTH_WIDTHS long, stepping to the same side as often as SEGMENT_STEP_PROBABILITY by SEGMENT_STEP_WIDTHS, its throw scattered by SEGMENT_THROW_SCATTER and falling by SEGMENT_LINK_DEFICIT where segments link, its trace wandering self-affinely (FAULT_MEANDER, FAULT_MEANDER_WIDTHS, FAULT_HURST). On Venus the plateaus' crests carry broken, irregular graben among the fold belts, the collapse's brittle graben some 18 km apart now partly drawn at 4,096 pixels; the tessera share is 7.8 percent and the event's pass takes 43 s.
- **F193 The polar terrains' fissures as one network, and their folds at the measured relief.** Each terrain the plume renewed drew its tiger stripes as a cascade of parallel fissures (F57) and its lesser cracks apart, a past episode's cracks traced with no index at all, so that none met another; and its folds, 250 m high and taking their strike from every fissure within 20 km, swept the terrain in coherent whorls, a fingerprint, where Enceladus's are ropy ridges some 100 m high (Bland et al. 2015). Now: (1) each past episode's cracks meet one another in the pole's frame before the body carries them off, as the present episode's do, with an index of their own; (2) the thinned zone's cracking saturates: a fissure through the shell relieves the tension about it as far as it is tall, the local shell's thickness (its stress shadow, Pollard and Aydin 1988), and a new fissure opens wherever the ground lies beyond every open one's shadow, until none does (POLAR_SATURATION_TRIES sites tried), so that the fissures' spacing goes as the shell's thickness, as joints' do as their layer's (Bai and Pollard 2000); each runs along the field, turning to meet open fissures and ending against them, its width from the lineae's heavy tail and its trough as deep for its width as a stripe's; (3) the saturated set is linked by branches that leave a fissure as a stripe's forks do (VENT_FORK_ANGLE_DEG, VENT_FORK_BIAS_LENGTH), bending back to the field until they meet the next, wherever the ground lies beyond every earlier branch's shadow - cross-fissures across the nearest fissure first, the way cross-joints run between a saturated set, made the terrain a wall of bricks; (4) a fold takes its strike from the fissures about it each weighted by its stress there, falling as the inverse square of the distance beyond a fold's spacing (an opened crack's far field), so that the nearest fissure sets it; (5) a stretch of fold takes a fold's depth from the heavy tail of F63 (POLAR_FOLD_PACKET_SPREAD 1, was the root of a third, a packet's mean), low most of its length and rising now and then; (6) the folds stand POLAR_FOLD_AMPLITUDE 100 m, the funiscular terrain's (Bland et al. 2015), the deep folds with them; (7) the vents' false light-blue tint (VENT_ICE_TINT) is gone. Each of Enceladus's terrains holds some 160 fissures, 9,000 to 12,000 km of them; the stripes stand some 260 m below the ground 6 km to either side, the network's fissures 160 m. The stripes, measured some 500 m deep and 2 km wide, do not dominate a global view: what does on Enceladus are its sulci (T6). The event's pass takes 141 s at 4,096 pixels.
- **F194 The polar terrains' margin belts from the spreading.** At a global view Enceladus's dominant belts are its sulci, ridges and troughs that bound its tectonized terrains, Samarkand's about Sarandib (its highest ridge 1.75 km, Giese et al. 2017); the margin belt that stood for them was a set of constants, folds 12 km apart and 300 m high decaying over 15 km, in 25 km segments shifted by a drawn phase, and it read as ripples, too even and set one inside another. The lanes are not the sulci: their grooves stand as Enceladus's ridged plains do (F61, 0.036 of the spacing, Bland et al. 2007), and the relaxation since spares them whole at their wavelength, so neither the relief share nor a tilt then relaxation gives a kilometre. Now the belt is the margin's, where the old ground takes up the terrain's spreading: (1) the brittle layer at the margin, the share of the lid that rifts (GROOVE_RIFT_LID_SHARE) of the elastic lid at the terrain's time, thinned as the shell is at the margin, buckles at its wavelength (TESSERA_BUCKLING_RATIO), as the tesserae's short folds do (F187); (2) the folds spread as far from the edge as the margin's lid carries a load there, its flexural parameter, and reach two of them in and no farther, the terrain's edge distance carried out that far; (3) an episode's spreading, the terrain renewed many times over, is far more than the folds can take up, so their relief is F187's least of the line-length bound, the steepest limb the ice's friction lets stand and the relief that weighs on the layer as much as its strength holds; (4) the folds are drawn as the lanes' and the coronae's faults are (F132, F133): segments that come and go, step across and taper, swaying and wandering round the terrain's edge without a seam, their slots as long round the edge itself as they are drawn (the thinned zone's circle is 1.7 times as long), fewer the farther in from the edge and between the belt's arcs, so that the belt thins by losing folds rather than lowering them all alike, and the fissures' folds give way to it as it stands; (5) each segment is a fold, not a fault's trough: a narrow crest with a trough each side that takes up what it stands above, as ejective folds are (F65), twice the amplitude from crest to trough, none deeper than the bound, a shallower one of the tail less; POLAR_BELT_WIDTH, POLAR_BELT_SPACING, POLAR_BELT_HEIGHT and POLAR_BELT_OFFSET are gone. On Enceladus each terrain's belt is 43 km wide, its folds 4.4 to 4.5 km apart on a brittle layer 0.87 to 0.90 km thick and 480 to 490 m in amplitude, about a kilometre from crest to trough, the low end of the sulci's 1 to 1.5 km; the strength bound sets them.
- **F195 An ice lid holds no more relief than its strength bears.** The Phase 4F gate check found Enceladus's relief, its figure removed, spanning -6.0 to +5.7 km between the 5th and 95th percentiles, where its oval basins stand at most 1.5 km deep and its figure departs from its ellipsoid by 350 to 400 m (Schenk and McKinnon 2024); the excess, +-4 km at 80 to 300 km, was the primordial relief (F137), there from 0.12 Gyr on: its 11.5 km lid held every load shorter than two thirds of its flexural wavelength, 247 km, whatever the load's size (F139), so that the loop kept its bands from 6 to 198 km whole for 4.5 Gyr. A load's weight bends the lid: a sinusoidal relief h of wavenumber k by a moment rho g h / k^2, which brings the lid's surface to a stress 6 M / Te^2; past the crust's strength (F15, 5 MPa for ice) the lid breaks, and what it cannot hold rides on the ice below, which keeps of it what its flow leaves, as the ductile share does (F139). Each band's share on the lid is bounded at that relief, approached softly as a layer's relief is (F189), the rest on the ductile ice; beside a renewing terrain the episode's thinner lid holds as the square of its thickness. A rocky lid's relief stands on its crust's compensation (F170), not on the lid's bending, and is not bounded. Enceladus's lid holds 0.25 km at 400 km, 1.0 km at 200 km and 4.1 km at 100 km: its relief spans -3.2 to +2.8 km, its 80 to 300 km band +-1.5 km (it was +-4) and its longest wavelengths -0.6 to +0.4 km. Mimas's swells go and its craters stay (-8.8 to +10.0 km becomes -6.5 to +7.1; its deepest crater 16.4 km from rim to floor against 16.7), Miranda's -4.6 to +6.5 km becomes -3.3 to +4.8, Charon's -4.2 to +2.7 becomes -3.7 to +2.2, Callisto's eases a little, and Europa, Ganymede, Pluto, Triton, Titan and Ceres change by a few percent (renders at 2,048 pixels, within 60 degrees of the equator). The bands below 80 km lie in cold ice that does not flow, so Enceladus's craters keep their depth: their relaxation wants the heat history of audit M3.
- **F196 The renewed ground keeps the base relief as it stands.** The user saw Enceladus's polar and fossil terrains carrying a finer noise than the ground about them, which looked smoother, and asked that they take the noise that would be in their place, worked as the cratered ground's is. A renewed terrain keeps the base relief below the length it levels over (F141), and every renewal, a lane's, a corona's, a polar or fossil terrain's, took it as the body formed with it, where the ground about it held it relaxed (F195) and worn by every crater's ejecta, which bury the hollows they can fill (F169): the present terrain, renewed at every step, held 1.6 times the plains' relief at 100 to 200 km and 1.4 times at 200 to 400, and every terrain a granular noise at 5 to 50 km that the plains' ejecta had smoothed. Only the ice shell's renewals reuse the base relief; no rocky process lays it again. The loop now keeps the base relief as it stands (LayerSet::base, where the surface renews itself): relaxed every step under the shell of the time as the ground is, its own bands held as the surface's, and levelled by each crater as the ground is, under its ejecta against its own level by the deposit's thickness, and inside the rim, under the floor's breccia and fallback, as the deposit at the rim levels it, so that a terrain later renewed over the crater finds it levelled as the rim's surroundings are and no disc marks where it stood (the floor's mean in its place, the first try, left flat discs with an edge at the rim). The renewals take it as it then stands; the finish lays a lane's or a corona's floor from it at the loop's scales, and from the body's own octaves only below the loop's cells, which nothing there has worked. The ground the stamp leaves is unchanged to the bit. On Enceladus the present terrain's relief at 100 to 200 km is now 450 m against the plains' 540 and the fossil terrains' 380, at 200 to 400 km 330 m against 300, and the terrains' noise at 5 to 50 km a quarter to a third less; Miranda's coronae at 80 to 300 km fall from 1.05 to 0.90 of the plains' about them; Ganymede's lanes, on a body of a few hundred metres' relief, change by little. A floor the finish lays takes the base as the loop left it at the present, after craters younger than its lane have levelled it, a little more than the loop's renewal at its time found it.
- **F197 The lid that holds a load is as old as the load.** An ice lid holding a load elastically was the thermal history's, down to the 170 K isotherm (ELASTIC_ISOTHERM_ICE), for loads of every age, and a thin plate that thick held every crater-scale load whole for ever: on Ganymede at 45 mW/m2 a lid of 4.8 km held everything shorter than 68 km, where Bland et al. (2017) find its craters of 10 km and more relaxed to their measured depths by 40 to 50 mW/m2 kept for a billion years, and Enceladus's relaxed craters want heat flows above 150 mW/m2 (Bland et al. 2012). Ice holds a stress elastically only where its Maxwell time, its viscosity over its Young's modulus, outlasts the load (the elastic thickness falls with the load's age; Watts 2001): the lid that holds the relief up now reaches down to where eta(T) / E equals the time the surface has stood, the time since formation, along the shell's conductive profile as the thermal history's lid is taken, with the model's own creep law (ICE_VISCOSITY_MELT, ICE_CREEP_ACTIVATION) and the rock's stiffening of the ice; some 130 to 140 K over a body's life. The faults, lanes and belts, quick against that, break the thermal history's lid as before. Enceladus's lid holding its relief is 7.2 to 8.2 km against 11.6, Ganymede's 4.5 km at 0.5 Gyr and 13.6 today against 9.5 and 34.2, Callisto's 3.2 and 8.3 against 9.4 and 33.5, Europa's 5.6 against 12.2, Mimas's and Miranda's as thick as before against their cold ice. On Ganymede at 45 mW/m2 the lid would let craters of some 16 km and more relax, near Bland et al.'s; at its own early 23 mW/m2 its craters of 20 km and less keep their depth, its oldest of 40 km lose a sixth, Callisto's lose four fifths and Europa's half. Enceladus's relief spans -2.6 to +2.2 km (it was -2.9 to +2.5), its longest wavelengths -0.39 to +0.23 km, near its measured figure's 350 to 400 m; its plains' craters keep their depth, its lid near 7 km all its life. Across the icy bodies (renders at 2,048 pixels, within 60 degrees of the equator, 5th to 95th percentiles): Mimas -6.4 to +7.0 km becomes -5.5 to +5.9, its longest wavelengths -1.3 to +2.3 becoming -0.6 to +1.6 and its deepest craters keeping 91 to 94 percent of their depth; Miranda -3.2 to +4.5 becomes -2.9 to +3.9; Callisto -0.38 to +0.22 becomes -0.32 to +0.19; Europa, Ganymede, Charon, Triton, Titan and Ceres ease by a few percent; Pluto is unchanged.
- **F198 A thinned zone's fissures run on until the thick shell stops them.** The user saw each of Enceladus's terrains outlined by folds and cracks running round its perimeter, the terrains near circles and ovals. A fissure leaving the thinned core turned to run round the margin (F43), toward the direction round the pole itself, so that every one that reached the margin ran on along a circle about the pole, and the zone's outline, a taper about the pole wobbled by a two-cycle field and structured at one cell's scale, drew near-ovals. A crack running from thin shell into thick meets a shell the same load stresses less: it slows and arrests rather than turning along the contrast. A polar fissure now runs on with the field into the thicker shell and stops where the tension no longer opens it (CRACK_STOP_PROFILE, as before), each where its own course takes it, so that the renewed terrain, which follows its fissures out as tongues, takes its outline from where they end; turning along the zone's own contours rather than round the pole, the first try, still traced the perimeter. The wobble of the zone's edge runs from two cycles round the pole down to sixteen, and the convection's structure from twice the cell's width, where the cells cluster, down to half of it. The circles of fissures that ringed each terrain are gone and its edges are ragged where its fissures run out; the pass takes 500 s at 4,096 pixels (the contours' first try 580). The folds that still curve with the margin, and the margin belt (F194), are the next part of the user's note.
- **F199 The margin's folds are the fabric's, raised where the spreading is taken up.** The margin belt (F194) was a set of folds of its own, laid parallel to the terrain's edge over the fold fabric at five times its height, so that its ridges crossed the fabric's and stood out from it, and ran round the edge tracing its shape (the user's note, audit W10). The terrain spreads across its fissures, as a spreading ridge does, so where its margin takes the spreading up the shortening runs across the fissures, and the folds it raises, across the shortening, run along them, as the fabric's do: the belt's folds are now the fabric's own. Each of the fabric's segments within the belt's reach of the edge (two of the margin lid's flexural parameters), inside it and in the belt's arcs, fewer the farther in, takes the belt's spacing (the margin's brittle layer's buckling wavelength) and its relief (F194, worn by the gardening as the belt's wavelength is) by the square of how squarely the edge there faces the spreading, the gradient of the distance to the edge against the segment's normal; where the edge runs along the spreading the margin shears and raises nothing. The separate belt, its segments drawn as the lanes' faults (F132), the fissures' folds giving way to it, is gone. On Enceladus the cross-folds are gone; the fabric's folds near the margins stand higher and wider where the edge faces the spreading, and nothing runs round the edge.
- **F200 Each reorientation fractures the shell.** Enceladus's cratered plains are cut by fractures (audit M4), where the tide stresses its uniform shell by some 20 kPa, under the 40 kPa at which ice cracks, and its thickening adds almost nothing: only its thinned zones cracked, so each terrain's fissures stayed within its zone and no terrain met another (the user's note, audit W10). The body turns at each episode's start to bring its thinned zone to the pole (F53), and a locked moon's figure, its tidal bulge toward the planet and its rotational flattening about its spin axis, a third of the tide's in the degree-2 term with its sign turned, turns with its axes against a shell that had relaxed to the old ones (Matsuyama and Nimmo 2008): in the body's frame each episode's spin axis stands where its terrain lies and its tidal axis is carried with it, and the stress is the figure's change, drawn with the shell's response as the non-synchronous rotation's is; turning Enceladus's spin axis 30 degrees stresses its shell by some 360 kPa, nine times the cracking stress. The stress grows as the body turns and the first fractures open where it is greatest (REORIENTATION_TRIES sites ranked), across the most tensile stress, running on until it no longer opens them or they meet another, each relieving the membrane stress about it for about half its length, as a long crack in a plate in tension does, so that the next opens only beyond every open one's reach. A fracture (the Fracture kind) is a trough with soft walls, in segments as a ridge's crack, as deep for its width as a ridge stands (LINEA_RELIEF_SHARE), with no stain; it is as old as its reorientation and stands in the body's frame, so later terrains renew over the older fractures and later fractures cut the older terrains. The cracks that open after meet the fractures as they meet one another, where the fractures still stand, in the old ground beyond the thinned zone, which renews itself over them: a terrain's fissures running out of its zone end against them. The folds follow the terrain's own cracks and the renewed terrain its own fissures, not the fractures. Saturating each reorientation at the shell's thickness, the first try, drew a mesh of 211,000 km of faint troughs and doubled the pass's time; the first fractures alone are some 20 to a reorientation, about 210 km long, 33,000 km in all, 0.3 to 2 km wide and 15 to 100 m deep: a reorientation's stress is great but the strain it relieves small, some 4e-4, so they are narrow and shallow, faint at 4,096 pixels and drawn at export resolutions. The pass takes 600 s at 4,096 pixels (500 without them). The belts that link Enceladus's terrains on its images, kilometres in relief, want far more strain than the fractures take up: a shell thinner where they formed, the heat history of audit M3.
- **F201 Io's lava from its heat budget, each lobe at its own time.** Io's flows were three fields to a patera, each the share of its reach squared that a flood's 50 m sheet covers, their ages log-uniform from a year to 300,000 and each laid at once with one age: 926 fields whose volumes summed to some 24,000 years of the lava the heat budget erupts, crowded towards the present, each field one shade, a flat dark blob or hidden whole (the gate check; audits M8, M20). Now the lava the budget erupts, the melt's eruptive share (VOLCANISM_EXTRUSIVE_FRACTION; 45 km³ a year on Io), is shared among the paterae by their floors' areas (a stated choice: Veeder et al. 2012 read the paterae's heat from their dark floors), each erupting a fifth of the time (IO_PATERA_ACTIVE_FRACTION) at the rate that keeps its share, some 20 m³/s on average and a few hundred at the largest, against Prometheus's 49 (Davies 2003: 3 to 300 m³/s across Io's volcanoes). Each patera builds its fields one after another back from the present, a field taking as many years of its share as its volume needs, decades at the largest paterae to a hundred thousand years at the smallest, its lobes laid through them in the order they were traced, each taking its share of the field's area, so that a cell shows the last lobe that covers half of it (`lobe_cover_laid`; the flood record carries the years its lava came out over); its last lava an active patera's erupting now, a quiet one's a quiet spell ago, memoryless at the duty its eruptions keep (IO_FLOW_SPAN_YR); back until the fallout about it hides rough lava five times over (IO_FLOW_SHOW_DEPTHS) or the plains' age, at most eight fields (IO_FLOW_FIELDS_MAX), some 2,100 in all. The fields cover 27.4 percent of the surface (Williams et al. 2011: 27.8). The first try hid them: rough lava hid under F84's centimetre of fines, which the plumes' fall about the paterae, 0.1 to 3 optical depths a year, lays within a few centuries, and lava showed over 3 percent of the surface; rough lava hides only once the fines fill its hollows, some 20 cm of them over a pahoehoe surface rough at decimetres (IO_LAVA_COAT_DEPTHS, 10,000 optical depths), and lava now shows over 15 percent, more than four fifths of the cell's ground over 3.8 percent (Williams et al.: dark flows and floors 3.4 percent) and the rest grey, mantled to middling albedos as Williams et al.'s undivided flows are, the youngest lobes dark over the older grey ones as at Prometheus (PIA02557). The pass takes 75 s at 4,096 pixels (50 before), the loop laying each kept step's fields. Still open: a field's sheet is the floods' 50 m where Io's flow units are metres (Davies 2003: 0.1 to 13 m; M20); Io's bright flows, sulfur lavas (Williams et al.: 4.3 percent), and its floors' range of colours; and the flows' white halos, which the plumes at their fronts take up next.
- **F202 The Prometheus-class plumes rise at the flows' fronts.** Io's Prometheus-class plumes stood at their paterae's centres, each eruption's deposit a ring whose range wobbled with the azimuth's second to sixth harmonics, so that every white ring was the same lobed flower (the gate check); and the SO2 a young flow drives off condensed within 8 km of it and stopped at its edge, a white line a cell or two wide about every young flow. Prometheus's plume rises where its lava runs over the plains' SO2, not at its vent: it wandered 75 to 95 km west with its flow field over twenty years while its vent stayed and its form held (Kieffer et al. 2000; Milazzo et al. 2001), and Io's white diffuse deposits typically surround its flows (Williams et al. 2011). Now the Prometheus class throws nothing from its paterae's centres: the lava laid in each bin of years over the history (FRONT_AGE_BINS, finer towards the present) drives off IO_FRONT_SO2 of SO2 for each cubic metre, the mean over Io's ground (Prometheus's ratio, 10^5 kg/s on 49 m³/s, Jessup et al. 2004 and Davies 2003, over the 16 percent of Io that is rich in SO2), which each piece of the source throws in the class's ring at the speed its place draws (a field's pieces and its plume over time erupting alike, FRONT_SPEED_LENGTH, with the class's scatter), drawn on a coarse grid (`front_falls`): the rings centre on the young lava, stretch where it is long, trail its growth and differ in size from field to field, and the flows' white lines are gone. Lava still shows over 14 percent of the surface. The first tries: Prometheus's own ratio on every flow buried the flows under white discs (lava showing over 5.7 percent), and the class's scatter of range averaged at every source smeared each ring into a disc. Still open: the rings are crisp white annuli, some 35 of them where Io shows a handful, its frost white wherever the fall outpaces the sublimation (audit M7: the model's frost brighter and more opaque than Io's coarse, translucent frost), which the frost's coarsening takes up next; and the plains' saturated yellow, against which the rings stand out.
- **F203 Io's frost coarsens as it ages.** A plume's frost falls fine-grained and bright and its grains grow from the vapour about them, so that an old frost of the same mass scatters less and lets the ground show through (Geissler et al. 2001: thin frosts transparent under normal light; Douté et al. 2001: Io's coarse SO2 at low latitudes, 250 µm to over a millimetre, where a fresh deposit's grains are tens of microns). The top layer keeps the frost that fell within about IO_FROST_COARSENING_YR (3 years, a stated choice: no rate is measured), relaxing to the fall as the old coarsens, in closed form; the rest of the column is coarse, its grains IO_FROST_COARSE_GRAIN (20) times a fine frost's, and shows as many times less for its mass, the column subliming its coarse frost first. It changed a tenth of a percent of the map: the rings that stand out are fed now, their frost young and many optical depths thick; the old frost it thins is Pele's and the small eruptions' at the higher latitudes.
- **F204 Io's colours are its measured ones.** Io's sulfur and its polar red-brown were stated colours, a mustard yellow (blue 0.45 of green) and a dark rust, under which the body averaged 0.44 in green and blue 0.52 of green as seen from its equator, where Io's geometric albedo is 0.74 at 557 nm and 0.28 at 404 nm (Spencer et al. 1995), blue at 455 nm some 0.72 of green, and 0.62 in V; against the plains the frost stood out too far (the user's note), and the plume's red, taken from composites that put Galileo's violet in place of the blue it lacked, was vivid where Pele's ring is a muted reddening. The maps hold reflectance on one scale (the Moon's map averages its geometric albedo, 0.12), and now the sulfur is set so that the body's mean is Io's measured colour (0.63 in green, blue 0.725 of green), the high latitudes a third darker and a little redder, and Pele's red red raised over green by its short-chain sulfur's absorption edge (Spencer et al. 1997); the hue in every band of latitude is the colour mosaics', whose brightness is a third under Io's measured albedo. The plains still lack the grey, green and white within them that the mosaics show.
- **F205 A plume's gas settles across its region.** Every gram of a Prometheus-class plume's SO2 fell in its ring (F202), so that the rings were oversupplied, white wherever their fall outran the sublimation, some 35 crisp annuli, and the ground between them had none: Io's white and grey SO2 covers 27 percent of it, mostly at low latitudes (Geissler et al. 1999), its strongest frost the broad fields of Bosphorus and Colchis Regio where the Prometheus-class centres gather, stable for decades (Trumbo et al. 2022). A plume's SO2 condenses only in part in its canopy; the rest joins the atmosphere about it, which the plume thickens over hundreds of kilometres (Jessup et al. 2004 over Prometheus), and settles as the atmosphere collapses each night across the region the day's sublimation-driven winds spread it over (Ingersoll et al. 1985). Now IO_RING_GAS_SHARE (0.3, a stated choice) of a front's SO2 falls in its ring and the rest settles across IO_PLUME_GAS_LENGTH (400 km, a stated choice) about the lava that drove it off, its mass kept: where fronts gather the gas outruns the equator's sublimation and broad, soft fields of frost form, the rings among them merging with them; frost shows over 40 percent of the surface, 41 percent within 10 degrees of the equator and 28 to 32 percent to 30 degrees. Beyond 30 degrees, where the sublimation is weaker, the gas lies thick, as Io's SO2 does there (Douté et al. 2001), and the plasma alters the frost on top as it alters the sulfur, so that it shows red-brown, masked as Douté et al. find Io's high-latitude SO2 masked. Io's frost is a warm white, its SO2 contaminated with sulfur (Douté et al. 2001), as the brightest ground of its true-colour mosaics; and with the frost ten times as widespread the sulfur and the high latitudes are set again so that the body's mean stays Io's measured colour (0.68 in green, blue 0.74 of green), the high latitudes three quarters as bright as the low as Io's true-colour mosaics show them, which the user found. The frost's spread of sublimation over the slopes went from six steps to sixteen, since a broad fall crossed each step in a terrace. Tried first and not kept: each frost subliming against the atmosphere its region's frost holds up, the frost cover times its vapour pressure over 400 km, without the albedo feedback (audit M7): frost went from 4.4 to 4.6 percent, converging in two passes, since away from the rings the gas the model laid was far under the sublimation. Still open: the frost fields are smooth over their 400 km, where Io's white ground is broken at every scale; and the frost's own temperature (M7).
- **F206 Frost does not hold on dark lava.** With the plumes' gas settling across their regions (F205) the frost fields covered the flows within them, which on Io stand out among its white ground as lobate grey-green forms (the user's note, against Io's true-colour mosaics), most of its old flows mantled under the frost and the larger and younger standing out. Frost kept off only lava younger than IO_LAVA_COOL_YR (20 years), the warmth of its eruption. But dark ground takes in some three times the sunlight the frost and the plains do (an albedo of a few hundredths against some 0.7) and so stands tens of kelvin warmer by day, its temperature going as the fourth root of what it absorbs, where SO2's vapour pressure is thousands of times the frost's own: the frost that falls on it by night is gone by noon, and only where the fallout has mantled the lava bright does frost lie. Now the frost shows only on the share of a cell that is not lava still showing (the top layer's lava, F84, F201), which takes in the warm young lava with the rest; IO_LAVA_COOL_YR is gone. Frost shows over 36.5 percent of the surface (40 before), the flows standing out within its fields as lobes of grey, the old mantled ones under it. Still open: the flows are a neutral grey where Io's are olive and green (Geissler et al. 1999: green material, sulfur altered by warm lava), and the paterae's floors black.
- **F207 The plasma alters fresh sulfur within months.** A Pele-class ring at the high latitudes wore a yellow halo where it met the red-brown ground about it (the user's note): its red sulfur reverted to yellow in two years (IO_RED_FADE_YR) while the plasma took ten to alter the fresh sulfur (IO_ALTERATION_YR, a stated choice), so that where a plume's fall outran the alteration and did not stay red, at the ring's thin edges and under the pyroclasts within it, fresh yellow sulfur showed. Io's red deposits at Surt and Tvashtar, 42 and 63 degrees north, were ephemeral (Geissler et al. 2004), going back to the red-brown about them, and no yellow shows about the high-latitude centres on Io's true-colour mosaics. Now the plasma alters what lies on top within half a year where nothing shields it, and the sublimation atmosphere's shielding is calibrated again (IO_SHIELD_COLUMNS, 10 to 21) so that the alteration still takes over at about 30 degrees: at 35 degrees it acts at 0.07 a year, at 40 at 0.3 and at 50 at 1.5, faster than the pyroclasts within a ring fall, and the ring there lies salmon on the red-brown. A first try at two years left the halo, the pyroclasts within the ring falling faster than that.
- **F208 A Pele-class plume rises from the cracks along its lava lake.** A Pele-class ring's range wobbled with the azimuth's second to sixth harmonics drawn at random (IO_RING_EDGE_WOBBLE) and stretched across its vent by a drawn elongation (IO_VENT_ELONGATION), so that each was a smooth loop owing nothing to its patera, its outer edge steep and its pyroclasts an even darkening within. Pele's plume and its ring are made by gas erupting from cracks and holes in its lava lake, a curved line source along the lake's edge reproducing the ring's features and the lake's small features its asymmetries (McDoniel et al. 2015); the plasma's heating inflates the canopy and softens the ring (McDoniel et al., Physics of Fluids); and dark fans of pyroclasts radiate from Pele's vent. Now each eruption cracks its lava lake's margin at IO_PELE_JETS places (10 to 20, log-uniform), drawn along the patera's own outline by length, each facing out across the margin there and throwing its gas over IO_PELE_GAS_SPREAD (30 degrees) about that facing: the ring reaches farther where more of the gas faces, by IO_PELE_MARGIN_SHAPE (0.2) of its range for the gas's full departure from even, so that an elongated patera's ring stretches across its long axis as Pele's 1,200 by 1,000 km ring does (Geissler et al. 2004), each eruption's own; IO_PELE_JET_SHARE (0.6) of its pyroclasts jet along the cracks, each within IO_PELE_JET_WIDTH (10 degrees), the dark fans about the vent, the rest falling evenly; and its ring's outer edge falls off over IO_RING_OUTER_WIDTH_PELE (0.2 of the range, a Prometheus-class ring's 0.1). The random harmonics and the drawn elongation of the Pele-class rings are gone; the small eruptions keep their elongation (IO_VENT_ELONGATION). Tried first: the whole margin's facing made smooth ovals, and jets of 5 degrees carrying every pyroclast black propeller blades about the vents.
- **F209 Sulfur falling on warm lava greens it.** Io's flows and floors were black lava under a yellow coat, where its true-colour mosaics show them olive and its paterae's floors a dark green, the darkest ground half a percent of the surface (the gate check; the user's note). Io's green material lies on its caldera floors and dark flows, its edges sharp, apparently a sulfur-rich coating formed on warm silicate lava (Geissler et al. 1999; NASA PIA02535, Culann Patera). Now the top layer keeps the share of its lava whose surface has reacted with the sulfur that fell on it while warm: a lava's warmth fades over IO_LAVA_WARM_YR (20 years, a stated choice: Io's flows glow in the infrared for years to decades), and the sulfur falling on it greens it whole under IO_GREEN_DEPTHS (an optical depth, a stated choice) of the warmth-weighted fall, integrated over each step exactly; new lava laid over it is fresh. The materials draw the dark ground from black toward IO_GREEN, the dark green about the paterae on Io's true-colour mosaics, by the reacted share (the `reacted` layer). The young flows and the floors under the plumes' fall are dark green, the older flows olive under their coats, and the darkest ground covers 0.6 percent of the surface (1.3 before).
- **F210 The atmosphere that shields the ground is held up over the frost.** With the plasma altering within half a year (F207) the shielding had to be steep to hold the red-brown off below 30 degrees, and the low latitudes' yellow ended at a line of latitude. Io's atmosphere is held up by its frost's sublimation and is densest over its SO2 frost, its equatorial column some ten times greater over the frost-rich anti-Jovian longitudes than elsewhere (Feaga et al. 2009). Now the history runs twice: a first pass over a ninth of the cells finds the frost, and the column at each place is its latitude's frost vapour pressure times its region's frost cover over IO_PLUME_GAS_LENGTH (400 km), over bare ground IO_ATMOSPHERE_FLOOR (0.2) of a frost-covered region's; the alteration takes two years where unshielded (IO_ALTERATION_YR), as fast as the red sulfur reverts, and the shielding (IO_SHIELD_COLUMNS, 23) is calibrated so that it takes over at about 30 degrees where the frost is as it is there on average. The red-brown reaches toward the equator where the ground is bare and holds off poleward under the frost fields, fading over some 15 degrees, and the high-latitude rings still lie on it without a halo. Frost shows over 29 percent of the surface (Geissler et al. 1999: 27). The pass takes 17 s at 4,096 pixels, the jets of the Pele-class pyroclasts drawn only within the third of the range they fall in.
- **F211 The plumes' gas settles unevenly.** The frost fields the plumes' gas laid (F205) were smooth over their 400 km, white wherever the smooth fall outran the sublimation, where Io's white ground is broken at every scale into wisps and patches with the sulfur and the flows between (the user's note, against Io's true-colour mosaics). The gas settles as the atmosphere collapses each night, over the ground's relief and in the day's winds: unevenly, as the plumes' far fallout lands (F89). Now its mass is clumped as the far fallout's is, by a log-normal factor whose logarithm spreads by IO_FAR_FALLOUT_SPREAD from IO_FAR_FALLOUT_MOTTLE (120 km) down to the grid's cells, its mean over the surface one (`clumping`): where the frost holds is still the gas's supply against the sublimation, and the clumps break the fields' edges and their insides at every scale. Frost shows over 27 percent of the surface (Geissler et al. 1999: 27).
- **F212 What a ray's secondaries throw out is mostly the skin far from its crater.** The Moon's rays read at a global view as some twenty bright starbursts, their spokes solid bands 15 to 35 km wide and as bright as a fresh crater's ejecta along most of their length, where its reference map shows faint fans of fine streaks over the highlands (the gate check; the user's note, audit M36): the model's brightest half percent of the surface at 0.254 against the map's 0.195, its 95th percentile at 0.194 against 0.163. The ground the secondaries excavated (F91) was all counted fresh, and Elliott et al. (2018) put half of it excavated where a ray ends (their P = 0.5, read on stretched images), so a ray was half fresh at its end and saturated within. In their account the ray ends where the secondaries no longer dig below the weathered skin, "the fresh material diluted by the weathered material": a secondary digging to a depth d through a skin h throws out its column, h / d of it the skin's own mature soil. Now the share a ray freshens is the share excavated times 1 - h / d, the depth falling with distance as the largest secondary does (SECONDARY_SIZE_FALL) while the excavated area falls as the RAY_DENSITY_SLOPE, the depth reaching the skin where the ray ends (the excavated area times the skin's share at ln 2), so that along a jet h / d is that area against the end's to -0.5 / 3.48 (`Rays::fresh_share`). A ray is bright near its crater and fades to the ground at its end, half its fresh contrast at a quarter of its length; the ray lengths of Elliott et al.'s fit hold as the excavated share's, and a ray now shows to some 0.86 of them. The rays are worked out only as far as a jet's fragments still dig below the skin (`fresh_reach`), not to where a burst could excavate a twentieth, which takes the Moon's rays from 13.8 s to 4.6 s. On the Moon 4.8 percent of the surface is brightened past a tenth of the fresh contrast and 0.4 percent past a half (21 and 4 at F91), its brightest half percent at 0.228 and its 95th percentile at 0.153; on Ganymede 8.5 and 1.2 (28 and 7), on Callisto 1.1 and 0.3, on Europa 3.6 and 1.0, on Mercury 0.1 and none (3 and 0.03): Mercury's rays, which fade in 0.15 Gyr, were already all but gone, where its reference map shows Hokusai's and Debussy's across the planet (audit M38).
- **F39 Viscous relaxation of an ice shell.** A load of wavelength λ relaxes on τ = 4π η / (ρ g λ) with η the viscosity of the ice at depth 0.16 λ into the shell, along the conductive profile T(z) = T_s (T_m / T_s)^(z/d), η = 1e14 Pa s x exp((60 kJ/mol / R)(1/T − 1/T_m)) x 10^(12 f) for a crust with non-ice share f; loads shorter than 4 times the elastic thickness ride on the lid and do not relax, and the share that relaxes rises to 1 at twice that. The band low-passes are area averages (a sampled pyramid let one cell stand for a patch). Where the shell is thinned (F43) the topography relaxes towards the thinned shell's equilibrium surface rather than treating the depression as a load, and a renewed terrain drops the wavelengths shorter than its renewal length and relaxes the rest with its neighbours. The loop applies the survival per wavelength band at every step with the shell as the thermal history has it; replayed craters relax by the same law over their lives.
- **F40 Sublimation lag and frost.** Ice sublimes at the latitude's mean temperature T = (S (1 − A) cos φ / π σ)^(1/4) at the Hertz-Knudsen rate of its vapour pressure (Murphy and Koop 2005); the non-ice share of the regolith stays as lag, which chokes its growth to the square root of time and is opaque past 2,000 kg/m²; each cell sits at its own temperature, the body's Bond albedo scaled by the cell's reflectance against the mature surface's, so a fresh bright surface is cold and keeps its brightness until gardening takes it (Callisto's young craters) while a dark lag warms and grows; clean excavated ice is gardened back in over 0.5 Gyr; the ice the equator lost condenses where the mean temperature is below 105 K, covering up to 45% there, in proportion to the square of the regolith's cleanliness (a carbonaceous regolith gardens the frost in, so Ceres and Callisto show none and Ganymede six tenths).
- **F38 Rift population.** On each plume rise the extension E = tectonics.rift_extension x (rise height / nominal height) is spent along a transect 0.7 x 2π a long as Σ heave_i x L_i, the troughs drawn from N(>L) ∝ L^−2 between 50 km and 1.5 rise diameters with depth 1.5e−3 L (log-normal scatter 0.4, capped at the uplift), width 30 D and heave 1.2 D; half of them join an existing trough chosen by length as an en-echelon neighbour 0.8 to 3.5 widths to its side. Troughs wider than two coarse cells are carved through the loop over 1 Gyr, the rest cut at the finish; the rifting follows the dome on a stagnant lid and the resurfacing event on an episodic lid.
- **F37 Steady-flux production.** After the heavy bombardment the lunar production function falls two cumulative slopes steeper above 150 km and makes nothing over 600 km; each crater draws from the early or steady population by the early share r e^(−t/τ) / (1 + r e^(−t/τ)) of the flux at its time (F31).
- **F34 Threshold friction speed.** u*_t = 0.1 sqrt(ρ_particle g d / ρ_air) for sand-size grains (Iversen and White 1982, large-particle limit); d = 200 µm default.
